# Go port working notes

- Goal: a complete Go compiler with equivalent behavior, then a measured 10× end-to-end type-checking speedup over the original native tsc-ts implementation.
- Original repository baseline: `d3e96c1`; native build uses locked scriptc 0.3.5 with the repository's speed optimization and detached-exit flags. Node execution is not the performance baseline.
- Starting Go implementation: maintained source fork of the exact compiler ancestor, Microsoft TypeScript-Go `2bd066d87f5bafd315be9f40889d0a60b9e58e0b`. Preserve licensing and distinguish inherited code from new optimizations.
- Target workloads from the supplied screenshots: VS Code, Sentry, TypeORM, tRPC. Screenshots provide no revisions, commands, machine specification, or raw measurements. Independently pinned snapshots must be identified as new measurements, not reproductions of the quoted ratios.
- Benchmark requirements: same sources/options/libs, cold process per sample, warm filesystem cache identified, no incremental result reuse, successful checks and diagnostics parity, alternating execution order, raw samples and toolchain/CPU metadata. No successful speedup claim for failed or skipped work.
- Initial audit: the old benchmark omits native tsc-ts whole-project checking and ignores process failures. A new correctness-gated harness is under development.
- Initial optimization candidates: checker scheduling and duplicated semantic work; profile allocations and GC before changing runtime policy. Full correctness is a constraint; do not remove checks to obtain speedups.
- GitHub Actions CI and release workflows moved to `.github/workflows-disabled/` at user request. Validation runs locally. Commit `3979390` pushed to main. Repository Actions permission also disabled through GitHub API and verified `enabled:false`; no outstanding runs were listed.
- Environment: Linux x86_64, 8 logical CPUs, approximately 39 GiB RAM; Node 24.15.0, npm 11.12.1, Go 1.26.9 installed under `/tmp/tsc-toolchains/go`, clang/lld 18 installed. Standard sandbox networking failed DNS; approved escalated network commands succeed. No environment-status tool or network-policy snapshot is exposed here.
- Toolchain setup: locked npm install completed; scriptc postinstall required `npm rebuild scriptc --foreground-scripts`; pinned upstream compiler and TypeScript corpus downloaded.
- Source checkpoint: `packages/tsc-go` contains the full pinned Go compiler, embedded libraries, original internal tests, npm build/launcher integration, and `TSC_TS_LIB_DIR` compatibility. Initial Go and native tsc-ts builds are still running at this checkpoint; compilation, behavioral validation, and performance are not yet claimed.
- Benchmark harness committed as `59093fd`; five integrity tests pass. All four snapshots/dependencies are fetched. Setup checks using the official TS7 dependency pass for tRPC, TypeORM, and Sentry; VS Code checking is still running. TypeORM needs explicit `module=node20,moduleResolution=node16` compatibility overrides, applied to both compilers. Sentry reference declaration builds are encoded in preparation steps.
- Initial builds completed: original native binary preserved as `packages/tsc-ts/dist/tsc-original` (SHA256 `525c0fe92b231e13ce225687929a606b1d7d47851216cc10d4e2b91dfdcd0bb4`); initial Go `packages/tsc-go/dist/tsc-go-initial` (SHA256 `82162d21b98442d33f6e47eb45381958a8ae0f322c5c6904623ee4f252fdeea2`). Both report version 7.0.2. Go source checkpoint `c6c77d1`.
- All six Go unit suites pass (core, ast, bundled, scanner, compiler, checker). Native parity: 65/65 pass. Existing integration: 11/12 pass; declaration union ordering varies with checker count, matching the TS-only fix in original HEAD `d3e96c1`; porting it is required. API tests pass. Native child-process tests require escalated execution because the sandbox can report EPERM despite child success; those sandbox failures are not compiler failures.
- First quiet-machine benchmarks (5 alternating pairs, one warmup; full input/diagnostic parity) show initial Go slower: TypeORM native 1420.04 ms vs Go 2878.69 ms; tRPC server native 345.89 ms vs Go 765.20 ms. Raw evidence in `benchmarks/results/initial-typeorm-trpc.json`. No speedup achieved yet.
- Exploratory profiles (not benchmark timings; other work was active): `/tmp/tsc-profiles/trpc-initial/24819-{cpu,mem}profile.pb.gz` and `/tmp/tsc-profiles/vscode-initial/27406-{cpu,mem}profile.pb.gz`, using the pinned reference Go binary. tRPC GC background marking ~21% CPU; VS Code pointer-map lookup and checker side-table operations prominent (~16% through LinkStore). Fresh instantiated-symbol ValueSymbolLinks allocation is a concrete hotspot; long-flow memoization is not the first target on these inputs.
- Optional deterministic cost-balanced scheduler implemented behind `TSC_GO_SCHEDULER=balanced`; compiler unit tests pass; default unchanged pending comparative timing and full parity. Initial binary preserved.
- Algorithm audit: final TS has lazy generic member instantiation (`63ae946`, `d7b26e9`, `a0dc2e2`), primitive-to-union relation fast paths (`c9d29b0`), and template instantiation/distribution caches (`b95d9ab`, `7df723f`) absent from the Go ancestor. VS Code profile attributes 7.14% CPU to eager member-table instantiation (overlapping its 12.06% enclosing member resolution); primitive union checks are 1.14% VS Code and 4.47% tRPC. These are opportunity bounds, not predicted savings. Go maps have no stable iteration order, so a lazy-member port must freeze or key keep/clone state, not directly copy TS Map position bitmaps.
- Larger initial benchmarks completed (3 alternating pairs, one warmup): VS Code native 21373.07 ms vs Go 46383.26 ms; Sentry native 14570.59 ms vs Go 42412.11 ms. Both have identical inputs and clean diagnostics. Raw evidence in `benchmarks/results/initial-vscode-sentry.json`. All four initial Go measurements are slower than native tsc-ts.
- Corrected candidate built and preserved as `packages/tsc-go/dist/tsc-go-corrected`, SHA256 `a54000fe92760b45e2cafe12f8bfbf9b0facd3d8d20a540699c1135ab0e51c64`. Combined declaration-order fix, fresh-symbol link allocation, and optional balanced scheduling pass core/compiler/checker Go suites, 13 existing integration tests (including the previously failing declaration-order regression), and 70 native parity comparisons each under default and balanced scheduling; no skips. Shared checker fixture extraction passes the original 15-case checker test.
- Status: next experiments vary candidate checker count and GC settings, with original native baseline defaults preserved and exact arguments/environment recorded. Primitive-union and lazy-member ports remain isolated/unvalidated. 10× target remains unproven.

- First runtime/scheduling sweep: eight configurations, each three alternating pairs plus one warmup on TypeORM and tRPC. Raw results `benchmarks/results/corrected-*.json`; all exact inputs and diagnostics pass. GC off with a 12 GiB soft Go runtime memory limit and eight checkers produced TypeORM 2005.24 ms vs native 1297.84 ms and tRPC 548.87 ms vs native 308.67 ms. These remain slower than native. One/two checkers and balanced scheduling did not improve these workloads; defaults unchanged. Three-sample results are exploratory, especially close differences between five/eight checkers. Baseline variation is visible in raw samples. Peak RSS has not yet been measured; memory-heavy tuning is not a new default.
- Large-workload tuning (3 pairs, one warmup, eight checkers, GOGC=off, GOMEMLIMIT=12GiB): VS Code native 20379.91 ms / Go 32534.85 ms; Sentry native 18906.07 ms / Go 47465.87 ms. Both correctness gates pass. This improves VS Code over initial Go but Sentry absolute times regress, with substantial native baseline drift as well; it is not a universal default. During Sentry an untimed process observation showed about11.3GiB RSS near the soft limit; this is not a measured peak-RSS result. Raw samples: `benchmarks/results/corrected-gcoff-checkers8-large.json`. No 10× result; native still wins all workloads.
- Next candidate (not yet validated/measured): lazy generic member allocation with frozen keep/clone state and deterministic name positions; primitive-to-union relation shortcuts; optional dependency-group scheduling; incremental JS require dependency fix from TS `136f140`. The require bug was reproduced on the preserved corrected binary: incremental rebuild missed TS2322 that a clean rebuild reported. Candidate source changes must pass tests before commit/performance claims.
- Queued next-cycle experiments remain isolated and untested: `/tmp/tsc-go-cross-product.patch` (TS `a4b0d33` guarded intersection pruning) and `/tmp/template-cache-port/template-cache{,-native}.patch` (TS `7df723f`, `b95d9ab` template caches preserving repeated error diagnostics). Independent static review of cross-product pruning found no blocker. Historical TS Sentry counts suggest these may reduce allocations; they are not current Go measurements. Go already hashes type-list cache keys, so no port is needed there.
- Broader correctness: pinned Go testdata and TypeScript reference baselines fetched; ignored fixture symlinks enable `internal/testrunner` conformance tests. Start with seven mapped/union/inference cases, then widen. That test runner requires an untrimmed Go test build because its repo path helper uses runtime.Caller. Original TS watch-memory supervisor behavior is another compatibility item under investigation.
- Algorithm candidate validation passed: core/compiler/checker units; 75 native parity cases under each of default/balanced/dependency scheduling (225 total, no skips); 13 existing integration tests; 26 non-watch build/incremental cases; all 366 library/package variants. Relevant upstream conformance passed 1,001 source/config cases, with 31 declared unsupported-option skips (17 ES5, 13 AMD, one outFile), and no failures after fetching missing JSX fixtures. This is a targeted subset, not full compiler equivalence. Preserved `dist/tsc-go-algorithms`, SHA256 `5bb0dde9b751bd003c2d3f9c9a002efb704dc192655cf7f99b36d2354f5d355b`.
- Watch limitation: two existing watch tests timed out; seven remaining watch tests were stopped after reproducing a directory-depth guard that installs no watches for shallow temporary projects. An isolated next-cycle patch also ports memory supervision and last-good-config behavior; it is not part of this algorithm candidate. No watch success is claimed.
- Next quiet benchmark compares the algorithm candidate at defaults, eight checkers with GOGC=off/GOMEMLIMIT=12GiB, and the same runtime settings with dependency scheduling. Runtime defaults remain unchanged. Architectural audit found repeated checker initialization/global merging and imported generic expansion; sharing mutable Go type objects directly is unsafe. Shared merged globals are being prepared separately; no predicted 10× gain is established.
- Algorithm candidate committed/pushed as `f42fbc3`. Three small-project configurations completed, each three alternating pairs plus one warmup, all input/diagnostic gates passed. Default TypeORM 2585.60 ms vs native 1361.69 ms; tRPC 585.34 ms vs native 320.07 ms. Eight checkers, GC off/12GiB, dependency scheduling measured TypeORM 1941.56 ms vs 1423.51 ms and tRPC 511.93 ms vs 350.32 ms. Small differences between tuning variants remain exploratory with only three samples.
- Algorithm default large-project results also passed all gates: VS Code 36538.58 ms vs native 19013.64 ms; Sentry 39583.98 ms vs native 20500.86 ms. Both improve absolute Go medians versus the initial port, with baseline drift between experiments; both remain slower than native. Tuned large-project run completed in `benchmarks/results/algorithms-gcoff-checkers8-dependency-large.json`; the quiet window ended before starting the next build batch.
- Next-cycle isolated patches, all static-checked but unbuilt/untested: `/tmp/tsc-go-cross-product.patch`; `/tmp/template-cache-port/template-cache.patch` and `template-cache-native.patch`; `/tmp/tsc-go-variance-inference.patch` (TS `6ca738b`, bounded target-argument guard); `/tmp/watch-supervisor-port/watch-supervisor.patch` plus `/tmp/tsc-watch-transition-tests/watch-transition-tests.patch`; `/tmp/tsc-go-shared-globals.patch` (TS `70365ed`, opt-in `TSC_GO_SHARED_GLOBALS=1`, donor-only diagnostic capture, COW symbols/private types); `/tmp/tsc-type-data-port/type-data.patch` and `AUDIT.md` (original concrete unsafe.Pointer plus immutable tag, nominal amd64 Type header 56→48 bytes, requires checkptr/race/parity/conformance and performance validation); `/tmp/tsc-go-harness-env.patch` (record native/shared-global knobs and symmetric explicit env removal). Cross-product, watch, shared-globals and TypeData drafts received independent static review. These are queued experiments, not claims of correctness or speedup.
- Environment audit: inherited TSC_TS_CHECKERS, TSC_TS_REGION, TSC_TS_RECYCLE, TSC_TS_SIDE_STATE and TSC_GO_SHARED_GLOBALS were all unset during current benchmarks. Binary hashes are authoritative; report repositoryCommit is measurement-time checkout, not independently embedded build provenance. Current algorithm source was committed unchanged from the preserved validated binary; future build-side provenance could improve automation.

- Tuned algorithm large results (three pairs, all correctness gates passed): VS Code native 27720.83 ms / Go 42609.20 ms; Sentry native 19418.44 ms / Go 47160.89 ms. Large native baseline drift makes cross-run absolute tuning comparisons unreliable; paired results still show Go slower. No runtime default changed.
- Cgroup metadata correction: at 2026-10-17 07:07 UTC the session belonged to `/user.slice/user-1000.slice/session-6.scope`, whose ancestor `/user.slice/user-1000.slice/cpu.max` is `560000 100000` (5.6 CPUs); visible memory limits are unlimited. The old runner checked only root cpu.max and missed ancestor quotas, although it did record Node availableParallelism=5. This applies to both compiler processes in this session; the historical reports do not independently prove historical quota values. New cgroup-aware metadata/counter collection is queued in `/tmp/tsc-go-cgroup-metadata.patch`, after the environment patch.
- Expansion/watch batch applied after the quiet window: guarded cross-product pruning, template caches, bounded variance-inference guard, watch supervision/config recovery/directory and symlink fixes, native transition tests, and environment/cgroup benchmark metadata. Trimmed binary build, Go units, native parity/integration/build-watch tests and full upstream TestSubmodule conformance are in progress. No new speedup or full correctness claim yet. Shared globals and TypeData representation remain isolated for a later measured batch.
- Full expansion conformance passed: 12,789 fully checked source/config cases, eight partially checked cases with inherited output exclusions, 2,118 unsupported-option skips, zero failures. All six requested Go unit packages passed after two test-only fixture corrections. Exact commands, fixture revisions, source digest and exclusions are recorded in `benchmarks/validation/expansion-conformance.json`; subsequent watch fixes do not change the checker. This is not a claim that unsupported options or skipped outputs were tested successfully.
- Expansion native comparisons passed 80 cases under each of default/balanced/dependency scheduling (240 total), and 12 Go integration tests. Earlier notes counted 13 integrations including the original JS-only checker driver test, which does not exercise Go; the native parity suite separately reuses its checker fixtures. Initial watch validation exposed stale cached missing configs and unrelated directory events from broad ancestor watches. Those fixes pass all 11 watch cases; final rerun also covers ancestor-only cache invalidation for same-path/same-time source replacement. Linux PID assertions now inspect every OS thread's child list, rather than assuming the process leader spawned a Go worker; both original native and Go pass the two targeted replacement assertions.
- Future Go builds write binary-bound `.provenance.json` sidecars with local source manifests (including untracked/ignored source), Git state, toolchain/build options and optional PGO hash. Benchmark reports verify present sidecars, explicitly mark absent historical sidecars, and reject provenance changes during a run. These are unsigned local records, not hermetic attestations. Eighteen focused provenance/cgroup/harness tests pass. Preserve a binary together with its renamed matching sidecar.
- Final Linux watch validation passed all 11 native cases and both deleted/recreated-project cases in three consecutive repeat runs (6/6). Additional fixes publish readiness only after watch registration, ignore obsolete termination callbacks, and suppress duplicate deletion reports only when config resolution previously observed actual absence. Corresponding build/watchmanager and ordinary-watch unit regressions pass. A separate missing-import probe produced the same TS2307 followed by no rebuild within five seconds in original native, initial Go and current Go; this inherited limitation is not fixed by this batch.
- Preserved expansion candidate `packages/tsc-go/dist/tsc-go-expansion` with matching sidecar, binary SHA256 `6749d967d1437efdbfe03479072a2352737900bc5057b27ee6730c1797fd9a06`, local source-manifest SHA256 `a89092fd6263a978b86700f99c704c560d2cd7d1c26671ae8b180fefd83ffde5`. The build-time base revision is `b4a01da` with explicit dirty source state; the manifest was verified identical to source being committed after provenance checkpoint `0e12d26`. Windows/Darwin compile checks are in progress. Next quiet run compares this candidate at defaults on all four workloads; no expansion speedup is claimed yet.
- Expansion source committed and pushed as `e0a3b27`. Windows/amd64 and Darwin/amd64 CLI test binaries both compile successfully (CGO disabled); their runtime behavior was not tested. Final native validation evidence is in `benchmarks/validation/expansion-native.json`. All builds and tests are terminal before the next quiet timing window.
- Expansion default benchmarks completed with three alternating pairs and one warmup per workload. All exact-input and diagnostic checks passed: TypeORM native 1214.84 ms / Go 2393.73 ms (0.508×); tRPC server 299.90 / 442.52 ms (0.678×); VS Code client 18842.54 / 34666.44 ms (0.544×); Sentry frontend 13088.42 / 33434.97 ms (0.391×). Fresh exploratory CPU/allocation profiles of the preserved expansion binary are next; runtime defaults remain unchanged.
- Fresh expansion profiles are recorded in `benchmarks/profiles/expansion-summary.json` with raw CPU/allocation profiles and commands. On VS Code/Sentry, LinkStore paths account for 15.04%/12.62% sampled CPU and GC paths for 18.72%/17.24%; global initialization is only 0.018%/0.09%, so shared globals are deferred. Individual concrete Type allocations are about 4–6% of sampled objects/bytes, making typed arenas a safer but likely modest future experiment. These CPU fractions overlap with callees and are not additive predictions of wall-time savings; sampled allocation totals are not peak RSS.
- PGO experiment uses the unweighted merged CPU profile `benchmarks/profiles/expansion/cpu.pprof` (SHA256 `f0c774f76ea6695dde72e179a4a5cbe34856b1f9386a0e67e8dc357094f2c19a`), dominated by VS Code/Sentry. Training and evaluation use the same four workloads; any gains will not establish performance on unseen projects. Source remains the validated expansion compiler while this build is evaluated.
- New isolated algorithm drafts: `/tmp/tsc-member-names-port/member-names.patch` ports shared property-name layouts and releases redundant materialized member maps (TS `d7b26e9`); `/tmp/tsc-conditional-inference-port/checker.patch` skips definitely-false conditional inference (TS `9946489`) with an independent expected-type fixture that passes original native. Lazy intersection/union predicates (TS `0d43400`, `e419e41`) are being prepared separately. None is applied, validated, or measured yet. Retention, reentrancy, symbol identity/order and diagnostics remain validation requirements.
- PGO build preserved as `packages/tsc-go/dist/tsc-go-pgo` plus matching sidecar, SHA256 `1dae86336c301870297fd92a352d4ec6d9ef4bb4d42a4d955c07747eb3a3d847`. - Untimed work-count audit: `benchmarks/profiles/expansion-counters.json`. Native default statistics omit worker checker Types; native Instantiations/Memory allocs are unpopulated and Symbols excludes checker-created symbols, so those counters cannot support Go/native work ratios. Single-checker tRPC reports 47,593 native Types versus 48,011 Go Types (+0.88%) with identical 942 files/lines/identifiers and clean exits; this is one workload's Type creation, not all semantic work. Large-project collection was curtailed (VS Code Go canceled, Sentry not run). No timings from this contended diagnostic exercise are entered in PERF.md.
- PGO default runtime benchmarks completed: TypeORM source + tests; Node20 compatibility native 1328.54 ms / Go 2404.94 ms (0.552×); tRPC server native 332.11 ms / Go 530.51 ms (0.626×); VS Code client native 18861.84 ms / Go 35280.05 ms (0.535×); Sentry frontend native 13419.49 ms / Go 32974.77 ms (0.407×). Three alternating pairs plus one warmup per workload, all exact-input/diagnostic gates passed. Raw reports: `benchmarks/results/pgo-default{,-large}.json`. Training and evaluation workloads overlap. - Native checker tracing resolves the instantiation-statistics limitation for a controlled single-checker comparison. `benchmarks/profiles/trpc-single-checker-work/comparison.json` records tRPC native 143,314 instantiations versus expansion Go 143,473 (+0.11%), and Types 47,593 versus 48,011 (+0.88%); both exit cleanly with identical file/line/identifier counts. These counters do not measure all work or establish results for larger workloads. Runtime/storage cost deserves attention alongside missing algorithms.
- Next source batch applied after PGO timing: shared property-name layouts with discarded pending graphs; lazy intersection never-reduction/unmatched-property predicates; definitely-false conditional inference guard. Combined isolated TestLazy/TestPending/TestConditionalInference tests pass after correcting the helper to call Go's free isTupleType function. Main-build, full conformance, API/emit and race validation remain pending. LinkStore insertion-cache experiment remains isolated in `/tmp/tsc-linkstore-cache.patch`; shared-globals and TypeData drafts are still deferred. A read-only scheduler audit identified library-resolution cost as missing from current checked-text-only dependency planning; no scheduler change applied.
- Initial lazy batch validation exposed one semantic regression: pinned `inferTInParentheses.ts` expected three `[number]` types but the broad conditional-inference shortcut produced `never`. Original native tsc-ts has the same bug; its matching behavior is insufficient to accept this regression. Six Go unit packages, 240 native parity cases, 12 Go integrations, 29 nonwatch build/library checks and three race-enabled parallel-emission tests passed, but full conformance failed that one case. Initial source digest `0e5c2e6741791925ad28d9c2c934d8eb280d51b0b9a30a3d9e26d4ede6c8453b`; production binary preserved as `dist/tsc-go-lazy-initial` (SHA256 `07a8d99c0c1c92ab750208564a9414c9465ba601f5e8fc125138c24bb0427a75`). Initial reports are retained separately under `benchmarks/validation/lazy-*-initial.json`. A conservative infer-independent literal-discriminant proof is being prepared; no baseline accepted and no performance claim for the failed candidate.
- Corrected lazy batch replaces the broad conditional-inference shortcut with a direct required literal-tag mismatch whose value cannot depend on infer. Parenthesized/nested callable, generic wrapper, constructor and non-strict null regressions pass; exact upstream inferTInParentheses now passes all eight checks. Full conformance again passes 12,789 fully checked cases, eight partial cases and 2,118 unsupported skips, with zero failures; all six Go unit packages pass (1,569 test records). Evidence: `benchmarks/validation/lazy-conformance.json`. Corrected production binary `dist/tsc-go-lazy` SHA256 `961e90a7e1d873477233c022716da08acc6f4b3f02df11c70d5e11360ca59ec3`, source manifest `39b165a093813ad622f9b276a3a9d6c433d0d6a91199ee6c897f3d8e3ed03a59`. Fresh native parity/API/emit checks remain pending. Initial race and scheduler/build evidence predates this narrowing and is explicitly identified by its original source hash.
- Corrected lazy candidate passes 80/80 fresh default native parity comparisons and 12/12 actual-Go integrations, zero skips (`benchmarks/validation/lazy-native.json`). All validation processes are terminal. Optional balanced/dependency, build/library and race-emission results are retained for the initial source and are not represented as reruns of the narrowed conditional guard. Next run measures the preserved corrected candidate at defaults on all four workloads with no competing builds/tests/profiles. No speedup is claimed before those measurements. Isolated future experiments: typed concrete Type arenas (`/tmp/tsc-type-arenas-port/type-arenas.patch`), LinkStore insertion cache (`/tmp/tsc-linkstore-cache.patch`), and cache-free Get/New split (`/tmp/tsc-linkstore-split/linkstore-split.patch`); all remain unapplied/unbuilt/unmeasured.

- Untimed single-checker large-project work comparison is recorded in `benchmarks/profiles/lazy-large-single-checker-work/comparison.json`, using preserved lazy Go and original native with exact unchanged benchmark inputs. VS Code native/Go Types 3,270,735/3,267,740 (-0.09%) and instantiations 4,208,367/4,217,548 (+0.22%); Sentry Types 1,792,408/1,798,696 (+0.35%) and instantiations 5,008,451/5,974,204 (+19.28%). - Isolated LinkStore instrumentation (`benchmarks/profiles/linkstore-hit-rates/`) predicts only 5.12% tRPC / 2.95% Sentry last-insert hits for the dominant ValueSymbolLinks store, versus 30.95% / 47.72% for SymbolNodeLinks. All per-store invariants pass. The instrumented binary includes atomics/registration and is unsuitable for timing or memory comparisons; results depend on default-four-checker scheduling. Generic last-insert caching is deferred. A direct Symbol any-payload design is also deferred: nominal amd64 Symbol size would grow 96→112 bytes for every symbol, and symbol→links→Type.checker edges change retention.
- Applied only the cache-free LinkStore Get/New split: map hits return locally, misses reuse existing New allocation/insertion. Bounded assembly verifies inlining into getTypeOfInstantiatedSymbol, with a 43-byte larger caller; this is code-generation evidence, not a speedup. Isolated evidence is separately scoped under `benchmarks/profiles/linkstore-split/`. Main production binary `dist/tsc-go-linkstore-split` SHA256 `1f7c8035c582176397ffcec949cbc38124160067a2dab20c10e9b3aca58c22ec`, source manifest `7a6e24817412d6dc121e517d453ac3978f19050409b19ac6145b86e03ee5b334`. Core race tests, compiler/checker units, 80 default native parity comparisons and 12 actual-Go integrations all pass, with zero failures/skips; reports `benchmarks/validation/linkstore-split-{units,native}.json`. Full conformance was not repeated for this equivalent container refactor. No insertion cache, direct-property materialization, Type arenas or direct-symbol payload applied. All validation/counting jobs are terminal before the next quiet benchmark.
- LinkStore split defaults completed: TypeORM source + tests; Node20 compatibility native 1385.96 ms / Go 2522.88 ms (0.549×); tRPC server native 337.86 ms / Go 533.80 ms (0.633×); VS Code client native 18036.43 ms / Go 34611.92 ms (0.521×); Sentry frontend native 12818.68 ms / Go 32116.51 ms (0.399×). - Reverted the LinkStore Get/New split after its measured experiment: no convincing benefit across the four workloads, with Sentry 32.12 s versus preceding lazy Go 28.81 s and nearly unchanged native baseline. Cross-run comparisons are not a controlled Go-to-Go causal test, but do not justify keeping the larger inlined code. Restored pre-split Get behavior; concurrent-read regression remains and focused LinkStore tests pass. Historical binary, assembly and performance evidence remain preserved.
- Active mapper-cache candidate skips key construction for newly active mappers, uses direct TypeId keys for alias-free nested requests, and allocates separate plain/alias maps only on successful insertion. Alias requests preserve complete keys and pre-worker lazy symbol-ID ordering; both maps retain existing active-only lifetime and inference invalidation. Recursive slice growth and same-mapper nested population refetch maps after the worker. Nine focused regression tests pass, including limits/diagnostics, alias identity and invalidation. Full pinned conformance: 12,789 fully checked cases, eight partial, 2,118 unsupported skips, zero failures; six unit packages have 1,587 passing records. The conformance report explicitly records a shell-wrapper exit-capture error after passing Go package records; numeric child exit codes were not captured. - Compact Type representation candidate: replace the two-word TypeData interface payload with the original complete allocation's GC-visible unsafe.Pointer and an immutable uint8 representation kind. All 24 shapes have compile-time zero-offset prefix layout assertions; six embedded-base accessors use checked kind ranges, while exact accessors reject mismatched allocation kinds. Production constructors supply the pointer and matching kind together. This never reconstructs a larger enclosing allocation from an embedded Type pointer; detached copied headers retain the original allocation and tail. Future factories must preserve the private pointer/kind invariant. Nominal header size is 56→48 bytes on amd64, 32→32 on 386; allocator/RSS savings and workload speedups are not inferred from this.

- PointerLinkStore experiment replaces only the checker-local ValueSymbolLinks map with a typed open-addressed table and retains the existing value arena. Typed key/value roots preserve graph lifetime and stable returned pointers through growth; duplicate New installs a fresh zero value while existing published values remain usable. Existing-key reads are read-only, writes need exclusive ownership, and independently mutating populated copies is unsupported. Address hashing explicitly depends on the pinned runtime’s non-moving heap; it never reconstructs pointers from integers. Other link stores remain unchanged.

- Saturated streaming length enables primitive-writer inlining. On the first overflow transition, stream() copies exactly the written prefix before setting n=128. Existing streams therefore fail all primitive capacity guards; full inline keys still use the unchanged overflow-pointer distinction for hashing. Byte-slice/string generic writers retain pointer checks. Serialized bytes, full128-bit hashes, lazy ID timing, stream identity and the no-copy contract remain intact. - User supplied OPTI_ANALYSIS_01.md and requested applying its strategies. Its numeric-key status is historical (that experiment is now measured/reverted), but the ownership/indexed-storage and cost-weighted duplication proposals are relevant. Prioritize a three-variant ValueSymbolLinks pilot: unchanged baseline, allocation-handle control retaining PointerLinkStore, and identical handles with sparse typed indexed storage. Include A/A calibration and a contemporaneous native milestone before broad claims; do not multiply past Go/Go ratios. The user subsequently requested no subagents; all were interrupted/stopped and further work is performed by the primary agent. The supplied analysis file is left untouched.

- Indexed-symbol architectural pilot is implemented as isolated, unapplied patches at `benchmarks/profiles/indexed-symbol-pilot/`, following the supplied analysis. It separates allocation-handle overhead from indexed lookup with three planned variants: unchanged baseline, handle-only control using the old pointer store, and identical handles plus sparse typed indexed ValueSymbolLinks. Handles are independent of lazy semantic IDs, assigned before publication in allocator-owned blocks, never reused, and fall back permanently on exhaustion. Typed keys/values, pointer-identity checks and fallback preserve collision safety and GC reachability; new storage adds no unsafe pointer conversions. Six focused overlay tests pass under each of normal/race/checkptr, zero skips/failures, with production source unchanged. Full binder/checker integration, full compiler/native/API/race-CLI validation, measured layout/codegen, A/A calibration and three-way workload time/RSS remain pending. This is architectural experimental progress, not a claimed speedup or attainment of the10× target. All pilot work is by the primary agent after the user stopped subagents.

Build settings exactly match the saturated baseline; source delta is exactly ast/symbol.go, new allocator and allocator tests, binder.go and checker.go. Five focused integration tests pass in each of normal/race/checkptr, including existing actual checker graph-retention/replacement tests. Root units pass2341records/ten packages/no skips; full conformance12789fully+8partial+2118unsupported skips/zero failures. Native80 and actual-Go API/emit/project12 pass with no skips; source/HEAD/pins/binaries stay unchanged. Native validation overlaps non-performance Go validation, and no elapsed times enter PERF. ### Argument request repetition census (2026-10-18)

Continued solo. The previous turn only acknowledged the subagent stop; this turn produced executable evidence for the repeated-work strategy in OPTI_ANALYSIS_01.md. `benchmarks/profiles/argument-request-census/` overlays production source 216d40c7 at HEAD 8363e16 without modifying production source/default binary. A bounded per-resolveCall observer keys ordinary candidate argument checks by argument/context/mode/relation pointers and observed flow/resolution/mapper depths. It neither reuses results nor proves complete semantic context.

Focused observer test passed; all 12 pinned workload controls (baseline, disabled, enabled across four projects) exited zero with empty output and unchanged physical input hashes. TypeORM repeated 707/119374 requests; tRPC 6/3284; VS Code 4982/2032194; Sentry 1587/498596. No cap overflow. VS Code had 99 repeated requests whose result pointer differed from the first; Sentry had 30. Pointer differences are not proof of semantic/diagnostic differences, and pointer equality is not purity. The counter also observed budget changes on 16518/1312/255187/104401 requests, respectively; it cannot attribute these to a particular nested operation. The map retains pointers during a call and can perturb GC, so these counts establish no timing or heap improvement.

Decision: defer this partial-key argument cache. Frequency is sparse and safe reuse needs a stronger state/effects contract; counts do not bound weighted CPU savings. Keep the original native comparison and 10× target unchanged. No PERF row, no production promotion. Initial sandbox provenance subprocess EPERM occurred before workloads and is archived; authorized retry completed. Next work should seek a larger measured architectural cost, rather than treating repeated pointer tuples as reusable facts.

### Lazy identifier-name materialization candidate (2026-10-18, validation pending)

Implemented a distinct demand-elimination candidate after the argument-request census: ordinary parsing no longer builds the identifier canonicalization map. The only runtime reader found is printer name-collision checking. Internal Go source API changes from the exported Identifiers map field to IdentifierNames(); CLI/API/emit compatibility is being validated separately. After rejecting lazy identifier names, audited a distinct full-name-flow storage hypothesis: canonical handles propagated through identifiers/symbols/query interfaces and built-in symbol tables, preserving map semantics. These are static overlapping sites, not dynamic eligibility or speedup budgets.

Pinned Go1.26.9 unique source audit: handle is typedpointer with canonical byte-string equality/concurrent Make/Value; weak canonical entries with eventualcleanup; string cloning avoids backing-text retention. Make still hashes/lookups, zeroHandle.Value is invalid, and pointer-backed descriptors add GC edges/allocations. No claim of pointer-free IDs, bounded cleanup delay or free conversion. Actual layout mirrors showName16→8,Identifier72→64,Symbol96→88,rawkey/value24→16, excludingcanonical/trie/cleanup/textallocations andrealmapoccupancy. ## Pending property type-cache prototype

At retained production HEAD bff6b8c, implemented isolated three-file overlay in
`benchmarks/profiles/property-type-view-prototype/`: a per-pending-clone read-type
cache, captured nameType, and transfer to eventual symbol links. Reentrant
materialization publishes the symbol into the cache entry; the outer query's final
assignment updates actual symbol links even after enumeration retires the pending
map. Actual symbols remain authoritative thereafter. Ordinary property write
queries preserve read-then-remove-missing order. No broad memoization or compiler
consumer yet; the rejected presence consumer is not promoted or rerun.

Worker scaling was interrupted on request during the first Sentry warmup. The three completed projects each have four timed observations per count. I verified the raw file hashes and recomputed their medians before adding the twelve rows to PERF.md. Four checkers had the lowest median wall time on all three. On VS Code eight checkers used 363 CPU seconds versus 260 at four, and elapsed time rose from 60.5 to 68.1 seconds. Sentry has no completed observation in this capture.

The next experiment keeps four checkers and assigns contiguous runs of checked source files by cumulative source length. It tests whether locality in the loader's order reduces repeated instantiation across checker-private caches. The existing dependency scheduler only groups selected large, checked import closures. Use the same experimental binary in both modes and measure the work counters before attributing a timing change to reuse.

The contiguous scheduler probe checked all four pinned workloads with four checkers and clean diagnostics in both modes. Default/contiguous instantiation counts were 425,723/379,592 (TypeORM), 222,416/201,448 (tRPC), 5,915,965/5,406,981 (VS Code), and 11,722,854/10,567,870 (Sentry). Each pair matched file, line, and identifier counts. Nearby file ownership reduced this operation count on every project. This does not identify individual interchangeable queries. Compiler package tests and partition boundary tests passed. Probe timing overlapped validation builds. The separate fresh-process elapsed-time experiment runs four measured pairs per project after warmup with round-robin and contiguous assignments on the same executable.

The 40-process contiguous ownership screen completed on November 20. Four measured pairs followed each project's warmup pair. Round-robin/contiguous median seconds were 3.186/3.701 on TypeORM, 0.641/0.677 on tRPC, 43.932/45.037 on VS Code and 32.444/32.791 on Sentry. Keep round-robin as the default. Lower instantiation counts alone did not predict elapsed time. A separate diagnostic build records per-file callback durations and instantiations by checker so the worker distribution can be inspected.

The diagnostic file-cost overlay recorded eight complete single-run callback distributions with matching file, line, identifier, type and instantiation totals against the corresponding counter probe. The busiest checker's share of total callback duration rose from 26.6% to 34.0% on TypeORM, 28.3% to 41.1% on tRPC, 27.0% to 29.0% on VS Code, and 27.5% to 31.3% on Sentry. This is consistent with a locality versus balance tradeoff. Durations include GC and descheduling. Parallel worker durations overlap.

There is a separate front-end opportunity on tRPC. The loader parsed 942 files, of which 834 were skipped for whole-file semantic diagnostics. They contain 90.9% of all AST nodes. The analogous node shares were 17.0% on TypeORM, 4.3% on VS Code and 21.2% on Sentry. A lazy declaration representation would still have to expose imported and global symbols, augmentations, parse errors and options. These node counts measure neither unused declarations nor avoidable wall time.

CLI comparison against the pinned upstream Go executable passed 77/80 in contiguous mode. The three differing outputs are declaration emit for the mapped-union-order fixture. A same-binary round-robin control on that fixture reproduced all three differences and passed the two other invocations. Commit 1758d98 introduced ordering to match original native tsc-ts for that case. Preserve the oracle difference and both logs. Contiguous ownership lost on elapsed time across all four projects and is removed from production source. Applying candidate.patch restores its source and tests for reproduction. Apply file-cost.patch on top for the diagnostic build.

A separate bounded binder prototype limits parallel binding to GOMAXPROCS workers, which claim unbound files from the program instead of starting one goroutine per file. The single-threaded path retains its existing order. The compiler package tests passed and the binder package compiled. CLI tests matched original native tsc-ts on 80/80 cases. Against pinned upstream Go they reproduced the same three mapped-union declaration-order differences as the round-robin control.

Four small-project measured pairs followed one warmup pair using the same binary, four checkers, extended diagnostics and GNU time. TypeORM binding fell from 120 to 50 ms, but median process elapsed moved from 2.746 to 2.777 s. tRPC binding took 42 ms both ways and median elapsed moved from 0.544 to 0.554 s. Keep this candidate isolated while testing the larger file sets.

The November 20 two-pair large-project bounded binder pilot also passed the file and semantic counters. VS Code binding fell from 1,042.5 to 863.0 ms and Sentry from 552 to 403 ms. Median wall time rose from 37.921 to 38.224 s and from 31.091 to 31.415 s respectively. Across this screen all four wall medians favored the original binder, with small differences and few samples. The phase reduction does not justify adoption. The prototype remains only in its benchmark patch.

The completed-import prototype targets repeated loader work across module references. Under the path's existing mutex it links a repeated import directly to a fully loaded task of identical spelling when depth and package identity require no further processing. A failed TryLock falls back to queueing. Multiple spellings, an unprocessed target, a lower depth or a new package identity also take the regular path. The candidate is an isolated Go overlay. Apply completed-imports/candidate.patch and add filesparser_reuse_test.go.txt to internal/compiler as a Go test to reproduce it. Build with Go 1.26.9 using the production project-wide inlining flags and enable TSC_GO_REUSE_LOADED_TASKS=1. The control is the retained production executable which predates this flag.

Compiler package tests passed with the candidate enabled. The focused reuse/locked-path tests passed under the race detector, and 80 CLI fixtures matched the production Go compiler. The official benchmark harness verified identical physical input manifests, pinned project revisions and clean diagnostics. Four timed pairs gave TypeORM 2,333.15/2,227.92 ms and tRPC 436.29/465.53 ms for production/candidate. Both the candidate patch and the separate diagnostic counter patch are archived. The counter build has separate output and timing.

File discovery with the separate counter build verified every listed path and its pinned contents in both modes. On TypeORM the shortcut handled 12,790 of 20,364 import and root tasks (62.8%), on tRPC 1,107 of 2,656 (41.7%), on VS Code 112,106 of 137,659 (81.4%), and on Sentry 71,903 of 108,212 (66.4%). These operations avoid queueing an already-loaded target. Unique inputs are still parsed and bound. Lock availability can change these counts on later runs.

The two-pair large-project import reuse screen passed all harness checks. VS Code moved from 30.806 to 31.757 s (0.970×) and Sentry from 26.715 to 25.899 s (1.032×). Bypassing most loader tasks on both did not produce a consistent whole-command gain. The small and large screens used two different sample counts, and each speed ratio compares only the matched processes in its own screen. Archive the prototype without adopting it. None of the three November 20 experiments meet the cold native-relative 10× goal.

The property-annotation demand probe walks ForEachChild syntax nodes after the check. In declaration files skipped for whole-file checking it partitions property signature annotations into disjoint outermost trees. A tree is marked requested when getTypeFromTypeNodeWorker saw its root or a nested property annotation root. The nested-property smoke fixture passed. All four pinned project captures completed with clean diagnostics and matching file, identifier, line, type and instantiation counts.

- typeorm had 6,893 trees without an observed root request, containing 21,211 nodes (1.63% of all traversed nodes). Keeping one placeholder per tree leaves at most 1.10% of traversed nodes removable through this mechanism.
- trpc had 25,226 trees without an observed root request, containing 86,405 nodes (15.10% of all traversed nodes). Keeping one placeholder per tree leaves at most 10.69% of traversed nodes removable through this mechanism.
- vscode had 37,495 trees without an observed root request, containing 145,320 nodes (0.70% of all traversed nodes). Keeping one placeholder per tree leaves at most 0.52% of traversed nodes removable through this mechanism.
- sentry had 62,186 trees without an observed root request, containing 250,067 nodes (3.24% of all traversed nodes). Keeping one placeholder per tree leaves at most 2.43% of traversed nodes removable through this mechanism.

These are structural ceilings for the specified trees in these executions. Binding, diagnostics and other checker entry points can inspect syntax without that hook. Node counts do not measure allocations, time or proven removable work. A property-only deferred representation has too little coverage on VS Code to justify implementing it as a cross-project architectural change. The next larger unit to investigate is the entire interface member list, including method signatures, with explicit accounting for declaration merging and binder reads. No timing was collected for PERF.md.

The interface-member probe covers whole member lists in declaration files skipped for whole-file checking. It records each source interface declaration observed through getMembersOfSymbol and each interface declaration on either input of mergeSymbol. The four report buckets are neither hook, member lookup only, merge only and both. The AST walk counts member subtrees once and excludes interface names, type parameters and heritage clauses. A two-file fixture exercised a merged interface and a file-local interface. All four captures passed physical input and semantic counter checks.
- typeorm had 87,608 nodes in eligible interface member lists (6.71% of traversed syntax). 52,591 nodes (4.03%) belonged to declarations seen by neither hook.
- trpc had 236,763 nodes in eligible interface member lists (41.38% of traversed syntax). 157,353 nodes (27.50%) belonged to declarations seen by neither hook.
- vscode had 375,048 nodes in eligible interface member lists (1.81% of traversed syntax). 171,409 nodes (0.83%) belonged to declarations seen by neither hook.
- sentry had 564,734 nodes in eligible interface member lists (7.32% of traversed syntax). 251,611 nodes (3.26%) belonged to declarations seen by neither hook.

The binder declares members eagerly, and the checker also has direct symbol.Members reads outside getMembersOfSymbol. A declaration seen by neither hook can still matter. No time or allocation saving follows from this count. Even eliminating every eligible interface member node on VS Code would affect less than 2% of its traversed syntax. Further expanding skipped-declaration laziness is a tRPC-specific investigation, while a cross-project representation change needs to cover checked source and semantic state as well.

The November 20 arena experiment targets a shared cost across parser, binder and checker. Production caps typed slabs at 256 elements. The candidate also caps requested element payload at 8,184 bytes, with one element as the minimum and the existing large-slice fallback. Size-class rounding can change actual capacity. The candidate keeps typed backing arrays and previously returned pointers live through normal Go references. A focused test covering zero-sized and oversized elements, clipped slices and pointer stability across rollover passed. Use the production project-wide inlining flags and compare candidate versus production with the RSS-wrapped official harness. The artifact is an isolated patch.

Byte-bounded arenas passed core and compiler package tests and all 80 CLI parity cases versus production Go. The first four paired samples per project showed median wall ratios of 1.037× on TypeORM and 1.032× on tRPC. Peak RSS medians were 891.2/886.5 MiB and 236.9/245.6 MiB respectively. Individual paired wall ratios ranged from 0.774 to 1.076 on TypeORM and 0.985 to 1.098 on tRPC. These small screens do not establish a uniform memory reduction or a durable speedup.

VS Code median peak RSS fell from 7,808.6 to 7,680.5 MiB and median wall time went from 32.516 to 32.388 s. Sentry fell from 5,394.7 to 5,375.6 MiB while median wall time increased from 26.565 to 27.975 s. Each large-project result uses two measured pairs with reversed order. The candidate remains an archived patch.

A separate extended-diagnostics capture measured the compiler's heap after its two forced collections. Matched file, line, identifier, type and instantiation counts held on all four projects. Post-GC heap fell 1.66% on TypeORM, 2.21% on tRPC, 2.20% on VS Code and 1.25% on Sentry. This verifies a modest reduction in retained heap across the suite. Byte-capping existing slabs does not deliver the requested improvement in cold elapsed time.

The November 20 front-end time budget uses fresh full checks with one warmup and two measured pairs per workload. The original native executable runs normally. Production Go runs its ordinary project check with extended diagnostics so its internal NewProgram interval is visible. This includes file loading, module resolution and parsing before binding. Go diagnostic process wall time includes extra reporting and forced GC and is excluded from the speed comparison. Both compilers listed the same pinned files, with content hashes verified before and after each group of timings.
- typeorm native wall time was 1158.4 ms, giving a 115.8 ms whole-command budget. Go program construction took 436.5 ms, or 3.77 times that budget.
- trpc native wall time was 343.8 ms, giving a 34.4 ms whole-command budget. Go program construction took 135.5 ms, or 3.94 times that budget.
- vscode native wall time was 23549.5 ms, giving a 2355.0 ms whole-command budget. Go program construction took 4417.0 ms, or 1.88 times that budget.
- sentry native wall time was 19360.8 ms, giving a 1936.1 ms whole-command budget. Go program construction took 3037.0 ms, or 1.57 times that budget.

Under these sampled conditions the current front end alone exceeds the total target on all four projects. A checker-only rewrite leaves this measured cost intact. Actual target attainment requires a full, same-workload, ordinary-command native comparison.

The direct-parent prototype specializes the parser's immediate-child parent-pointer assignments. Its overlay generator reads the current AST ForEachChild dispatch and recognizes only traversals expressed as a sequence of optional children, node lists and modifier lists. The 163 recognized dispatch arms assign parents directly. Four custom arms return to the existing visitor, and the original dispatch's leaf default has no children. The compiler's other visitors and AST representation are unchanged. The fixture exercises generics, lists, optional access, class members and JSX and compares each node's immediate parent links with the original visitor. Its initial path was relative and failed the parser's absolute-path precondition. The corrected fixture and the AST, parser and compiler packages passed.

Direct parser parent links matched all 80 production Go CLI fixtures. Four measured full-command pairs on TypeORM and tRPC gave ratios of 0.999× and 0.994×. Two measured pairs on VS Code gave 1.103× (individual ratios 1.184 and 1.023) and on Sentry 0.989× (1.019 and 0.959). The separate four-pair standalone discovery median ratios were 0.997×, 0.986×, 1.004× and 1.022× respectively, with identical file lists. The targeted phase has little measured improvement on VS Code. The short whole-command screen cannot attribute its 10.3% speedup to cheaper parsing. Keep the candidate isolated. The budget measurements favor changes that remove substantial front-end work alongside type checking.

The front-end CPU attribution reuses the four retained production raw profiles and verifies their hashes and total sampled CPU. It anchors samples on NewProgram and the file loader, parser task and project-reference worker stacks. The table below is conditional on those identified stacks. Separate GC and scheduling work may be outside them. Source parsing requires both the compiler host read/parse entry and ParseSourceFile. Within that category the script assigns a sample to the first matching rule, so scanner, identifier and parent-link activity are counted separately before AST construction.
- typeorm source parsing accounted for 62.0% of anchored CPU and module/package resolution 20.9%. AST construction accounted for 68.5% of parsing CPU. Within that construction category 57.4% had mallocgc or growslice on stack.
- trpc source parsing accounted for 75.6% of anchored CPU and module/package resolution 15.2%. AST construction accounted for 73.4% of parsing CPU. Within that construction category 62.9% had mallocgc or growslice on stack.
- vscode source parsing accounted for 80.8% of anchored CPU and module/package resolution 7.9%. AST construction accounted for 73.5% of parsing CPU. Within that construction category 53.9% had mallocgc or growslice on stack.
- sentry source parsing accounted for 58.7% of anchored CPU and module/package resolution 23.3%. AST construction accounted for 59.0% of parsing CPU. Within that construction category 55.3% had mallocgc or growslice on stack.

Constructor attribution includes callees and does not quantify avoidable CPU or wall time. Identifier construction was the largest named factory contributor on every workload. This motivates testing allocation sharing at compiler-host scope while preserving typed references and exclusive builder ownership.

The host-parser-arenas overlay gives each compiler host an exclusive parser pool. A parser returned to this pool clears per-file state and factory counters while preserving the typed arenas' current slabs. Subsequent files can allocate into unused slots. No published slot is recycled. Ordinary standalone parsing retains its original pooling path. AST, parser and compiler package tests passed, including a concurrent comparison against fresh parsing across TS, TSX, JS, JSON and invalid source with a previously published tree kept live. Sharing a slab can prolong the lifetime of an earlier file's graph when a later file survives, so short-lived CLI memory and incremental/API retention are separate questions.

The concurrent host parser fixture passed under the Go race detector. A separate weak-pointer fixture passed three repetitions. Keeping only a second identifier rooted allowed the earlier tree's parent to be collected with separate factories, while a shared identifier slab kept that parent alive across two collections. The fixture establishes a real retention edge. It does not establish its frequency in watch or API sessions.

Host-local parser arenas matched production Go on all 80 CLI fixtures. Four measured pairs gave TypeORM 0.918× and tRPC 1.056×. Two measured pairs gave VS Code 1.009× and Sentry 1.046×. Median peak RSS fell on all four, including 584.8 MiB on VS Code and 372.4 MiB on Sentry. Separate extended diagnostics recorded these heap and allocation deltas after the compiler forced two collections.
- typeorm post-GC heap -5.67%, cumulative allocation count -1.07%.
- trpc post-GC heap -3.99%, cumulative allocation count -2.48%.
- vscode post-GC heap -6.10%, cumulative allocation count -2.11%.
- sentry post-GC heap -4.46%, cumulative allocation count -1.44%.

The candidate reduces allocation footprint but has mixed whole-command timing, including a TypeORM regression. Its cross-file retention edge also needs lifecycle-specific handling before use in long-lived hosts. Archive the prototype and retain production defaults. The cold 10× native-relative goal remains unmet.

The fast arena reservation candidate moves only single-element slab growth into a non-inlined helper. Element size, capacity sequence, allocator, publication and lifetime semantics stay identical. Production disassembly of Parser.newIdentifier shows an out-of-line Arena.New call. In a matched microbenchmark constructing a chain of 1,024 node-sized records the production loop calls Arena.New and the candidate loop calls only growForOne on exhaustion. The chain integrity test passed. Six measured pairs followed one warmup pair on one CPU. Median batch time fell from 26,241.5 to 24,518.5 ns, with the same 78,512 bytes and ten allocations per batch. The microbenchmark is not a compiler performance measurement.

Retained production CPU stacks with an Arena.New frame accounted for 9.17% of TypeORM, 17.54% of tRPC, 10.21% of VS Code and 4.03% of Sentry. The slices.Grow source line alone accounted for 9.07%, 17.45%, 10.07% and 3.93% respectively. This attribution includes growth callees. A call-site inlining change cannot be credited with eliminating the allocation work in those samples. Source attribution does not measure the latency of function calls or indirect effects on generated callers. The full compiler result is required.

In the full candidate executable createIdentifierWithDiagnostic calls the identifier factory directly and that factory calls growForOne on exhaustion. The production executable calls Parser.newIdentifier, which calls Arena.New. NewPropertyAccessExpression calls Arena.New in production and growForOne in the candidate. The shift in inlining boundaries is preserved in compiler-codegen.json. All 80 CLI fixtures matched production Go. Small-project full-check ratios after four pairs were 0.983× on TypeORM and 1.039× on tRPC, with both workloads showing paired observations on either side of 1×.

The final full-check pairs on VS Code were 0.983× and 1.111× and on Sentry 0.954× and 1.080×. Ratios of median times were 1.045× and 1.014× respectively. Every workload had paired results on both sides of 1×. Keep the inline-only candidate isolated. Exact-content deduplication also looks too narrow for this suite. After rehashing the inputs from the four reports, duplicate copies beyond the first represented 0.57%, 0.29%, 0.07% and 0.11% of input bytes on TypeORM, tRPC, VS Code and Sentry respectively. These byte shares describe the inputs and do not measure parsing time.

An identifier flow-field observer counted distinct objects at two points in each full check. The binder marks an identifier immediately after storing its current flow. The first subsequent call to Node.FlowNodeData on that identifier counts one accessor visit, including presence checks. Atomic state on the identifier prevents repeated or concurrent visits from inflating the distinct count. Unbound synthetic identifiers are outside this denominator. The observer does not measure reads of identifier text, the need for the identifier itself, or demand from subsequent language-service requests.

| Project | Bound identifiers | Accessor visited | Share visited | Raw pointer bytes in unvisited slots (MiB) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| TypeORM | 427,689 | 127,333 | 29.77% | 2.29 |
| tRPC | 185,507 | 5,656 | 3.05% | 1.37 |
| VS Code | 7,120,230 | 2,362,289 | 33.18% | 36.30 |
| Sentry | 2,504,113 | 752,291 | 30.04% | 13.37 |

All bound identifiers had non-nil flow at binding, so a check for nil at the write site cannot remove these stores. The last column uses eight bytes per slot on amd64, before allocator effects and the cost of replacement storage. A sparse table still needs a way to find the correct flow on first access. On VS Code the raw payload of *all* bound identifier flow slots is 54.32 MiB against roughly 7.5 GiB measured peak RSS in the preceding production runs. Leave the production layout in place. This experiment favors studying whole-subtree or cross-phase work over moving this field by itself. The four runs verified pinned commits and input hashes and exited successfully with empty diagnostic output. Counts and the observer patch are in benchmarks/profiles/identifier-flow-demand. Observer timings are excluded from PERF.md.

The observer also matched production Go on all 80 CLI parity cases, including diagnostics and emitted files.

The keyword-dispatch candidate generates an exact string switch from the scanner's keyword map and a ten-spelling switch from the AST future-reserved range plus await. Binding ordinary identifiers can bypass the parent-name check and second general keyword lookup. The scanner and binder package tests passed, including the full keyword enumeration, mutations, casing and Unicode near misses. All 80 CLI fixtures matched production Go. Five targeted contexts including escaped implements, legal keyword properties and illegal module bindings matched both production Go and pinned upstream Go. The resulting compiler remains an isolated overlay in benchmarks/profiles/keyword-dispatch.

The union of retained CPU samples with GetIdentifierToken or checkContextualIdentifier on stack was 0.25% TypeORM, 0.43% tRPC, 0.50% VS Code and 0.51% Sentry. The profile counts include callees and count overlap once. In six measured full-check pairs following a warmup, TypeORM median was 3163.21/3163.28 ms and tRPC was 612.64/599.24 ms (production/candidate). Individual paired ratios ranged from 0.908 to 1.291 and 0.807 to 1.121 respectively. Median peak RSS rose on both. Archive this candidate after the small-project screen. Its direct CPU coverage is too low to warrant a larger screen for the order-of-magnitude goal.

Four alternating measured pairs after a warmup compared native and Go file discovery (--listFilesOnly). Each child was a new process. The capture verified the two pinned binaries, project revision, source contents and file list. This measures loading and printing paths with a warm filesystem cache. Native/Go median elapsed milliseconds were 383/615 TypeORM, 145/192 tRPC, 4627/6132 VS Code and 2192/3225 Sentry. Median major faults were zero throughout. On VS Code the two processes had comparable peak RSS but Go recorded 1,097,487 minor faults versus 260,734 for native. Sentry also showed a substantial difference. Fault counts alone do not identify allocation strategy or how much time was spent servicing faults. The binary-relative results in PERF.md are file discovery only.

The isolated Linux runtime overlay advises huge pages only for newly mapped Go heap regions, using the existing aligned sysHugePageOS helper. GODEBUG=disablethp retains precedence. No kernel settings were changed. Under this host's madvise policy, separate untimed VS Code discovery runs sampled /proc/PID/smaps_rollup every 100 ms. Maximum observed AnonHugePages was 2,609,152 KiB native, zero in the production Go samples, and 2,326,528 KiB in the advised Go samples. These are sampled maxima, and the rollup includes all mappings in each process.

Four measured production/advised discovery pairs per workload followed one warmup. Minor faults fell by 65–79% but median wall time rose by 5–6% across all four. VS Code median system CPU rose from 5.395 to 8.165 seconds, and median peak RSS rose from 3,560,864 to 3,723,138 KiB. Fault counts alone therefore do not explain the native elapsed advantage. The separate complete type-check screen tests whether later repeated heap accesses recover the up-front cost. The build uses Go 1.26.9 and the same project-wide inlining flags as production with heap_overlay.py supplying the runtime overlay.

The heap-advice build passed 80/80 production Go CLI fixtures. Four alternating measured whole-command pairs after warmup on TypeORM and tRPC gave ratios of median times of 1.030× and 1.071×. The TypeORM individual pairs ranged from 0.991× to 1.076× and tRPC from 1.015× to 1.272×. Both increased median peak RSS, by 17.1 MiB and 18.3 MiB respectively.

The full-check VS Code median improved from 36.979 to 32.413 seconds across two measured pairs (1.125× and 1.155×). Sentry improved from 29.041 to 25.847 seconds (1.123× and 1.124×). Median peak RSS rose by 38.6 MiB on VS Code and fell by 40.1 MiB on Sentry. Neither full-check result directly measures TLB effects. In a separate VS Code discovery run, the identical candidate binary with GODEBUG=disablethp=1 produced the same path list and reported zero anonymous huge pages at every sample. Retain the candidate for independent full-command confirmation with reversed starting order and a matched-source rebuild before adopting a toolchain change. The resulting Go/Go ratios are independent of the native-relative goal.

For the heap huge-page confirmation the control and advised executables were rebuilt from the same 837d06b checkout with Go 1.26.9, -buildvcs=false, -trimpath and the same project-wide inlining flags. Their go version -m metadata matches. The advised executable uses the isolated Linux heap-mapping overlay. The harness baseline slot holds the advised executable so it starts the first pair, reversing the pilot. PERF.md presents control/advised ratios throughout. The confirmation recorder validates binary hashes, pinned inputs, command-line equality, alternating order, clean diagnostics and resource-wrapper verification.

Eight measured pairs per small project gave TypeORM 1.036× (paired median 1.018×) and tRPC 0.977× (paired median 0.957×). The pilot's tRPC improvement did not repeat. Median peak RSS rose from 882.2 to 906.5 MiB on TypeORM and from 245.1 to 255.5 MiB on tRPC.

Four matched large-project pairs gave VS Code 32.409/30.086 s, or 1.077×, and Sentry 36.436/34.007 s, or 1.071×. Three of four VS Code pairs and all four Sentry pairs favored advised mappings. The respective paired medians were 1.079× and 1.045×. The VS Code session-cgroup median CPU usage also fell (143.19 to 134.05 CPU seconds). On Sentry session-cgroup user CPU was nearly identical (134.02 and 134.05 seconds) while median system CPU fell from 15.59 to 12.80 seconds. Session totals can include other processes. They do not isolate processor stall or kernel costs. Since the small tRPC result reversed, retain unconditional advice as an experiment. Test deferring the hint until the runtime already considers the heap large enough to enable huge pages for its metadata.

A separate size-aware runtime overlay requests huge pages on new heap mappings only after Go enables huge pages for heap metadata. In Go 1.26.9 this happens at a GC boundary when the heap goal exceeds 1 GiB. The overlay verifies that both heap-tagged mapping sites are in mheap.grow, which requires the heap lock. The metadata flag is set under the same lock. Older mappings retain their existing policy.

Untimed full checks with named Linux mappings enabled verified all pinned input contents and clean diagnostics. Sampling /proc/PID/smaps found no huge-page advice on Go heap mappings on TypeORM or tRPC. VS Code and Sentry reached sampled maxima of 7,094,272 and 4,050,944 KiB of advised Go heap mappings, of which up to 5,019,648 and 3,344,384 KiB respectively were actually backed by anonymous huge pages (separate maxima). The candidate also matched the original native compiler on 80 CLI fixtures. These observations establish size-dependent behavior on this host. The elapsed-time results for unconditional advice cannot be transferred to this candidate.

An offline four-worker scheduling model used the retained default-scheduler file callback durations. Holding every file's observed duration fixed, the longest worker must take at least the larger of total duration / 4 and the longest file. Dividing the observed busiest worker by this bound gave ceilings of 1.062× TypeORM, 1.132× tRPC, 1.081× VS Code and 1.101× Sentry for these callbacks alone. Assigning checked files longest-estimated-first by AST node count gave modeled ratios 1.019×, 1.017×, 1.019× and 0.956×. Assigning with perfect knowledge of measured durations nearly reached each bound. Real reassignments can change cache reuse, duplicated work, GC and scheduling. The model does not bound optimizations that change the amount of work per file. It does rule out a tenfold callback gain from redistribution alone under the stated fixed-work assumption.

The size-aware candidate completed a direct three-way cold-check screen against both the matched control and the original native executable. Each compiler ran once per block, with a warmup excluded and equal counts in each execution position. There were six measured blocks on TypeORM and tRPC, three on VS Code and Sentry. Every timed process exited cleanly with empty output. All three listed the pinned file set; physical input hashes and workload revisions were verified before and after. The three-way report includes elapsed time, process CPU, peak RSS and page faults.

Relative to the Go control, the size-aware candidate measured 1.025× TypeORM, 1.022× tRPC, 1.086× VS Code and 1.173× Sentry. The small differences on TypeORM and tRPC cannot be credited to huge-page backing since none was observed on those projects in the earlier mapping probe. On Sentry the median minor fault counts were 510,713 native, 1,664,301 control and 508,626 size-aware. Even at comparable fault counts, native used 57.46 user CPU seconds and the size-aware compiler used 93.29. The native-relative elapsed ratios were 0.554×, 0.681×, 0.644× and 0.516×. Reaching the 10× goal still requires roughly 14.7–19.4× shorter time from this build.

The experimental binary can be reproduced from the checkout with GO pointing to Go 1.26.9 and python3 benchmarks/profiles/large-heap-page-advice/build.py. This rebuild produced packages/tsc-go/dist/tsc-go-large-heap-pages and was byte-identical to the timed candidate. It reported version 7.0.2. The Linux runtime change is available for local use without promoting it to the default compiler for other hosts or long-running sessions.

Fresh Sentry CPU profiles used the matched Go control and byte-verified size-aware executable, normal cold-check arguments plus --pprofDir and the pinned source files. Each exited successfully. The size-aware profile sampled 119.04 CPU seconds. Across all type specializations PointerLinkStore.slot accounted for 5.05 flat CPU seconds, including 3.84 seconds in the largest specialization. Its most frequent checker caller was getTypeOfMappedSymbol. The control sampled 118.90 CPU seconds including 5.25 seconds flat in slot. These single instrumented runs do not estimate the elapsed effect of huge pages. A separate 10 ms gprofng clock sample of the original native Sentry check exited cleanly. Native's highest exclusive application symbol was sc_map_entry_f64_hashed (3.57 of 87.65 sampled CPU seconds); LinkTable.get also appeared (1.16 seconds flat). The compilers use different storage and profiling tools; similarly named frames need not represent equal work.

The grouped-probe candidate replaces linear scanning in the existing typed pointer link table. Hash, table capacity, 75% load threshold, identity semantics and arena value pointers remain the same. The candidate scans four contiguous slots and advances between groups by triangular offsets, which visit each group once modulo the power-of-two group count. At 75% occupancy a fixed synthetic uniform-hash trace on 65,536 slots reduced mean successful probes from 2.42 to 2.24 and unsuccessful probes from 7.72 to 5.98. This model does not include cache misses, branches, real key reuse or compiler time. The existing collision, replacement, nil-key, GC retention and concurrent-read tests passed normally and under the race detector. The full compiler benchmark uses size-aware page advice on both sides.

Four alternating measured pairs after warmup compared grouped probes against the same size-aware Go build with linear probes. Both projects verified pinned input manifests and clean diagnostics, and the candidate matched original native on all 80 CLI fixtures. Median Sentry elapsed improved from 36.018 to 34.978 seconds (1.030×). All four ratios favored grouping, though the final pair was unusually slow on both sides (45.09 s candidate, 66.08 s reference). TypeORM instead moved from 4.422 to 4.552 seconds (0.971×), with individual ratios ranging from 0.554× to 1.204×. Keep the probe policy experimental. The lower synthetic miss count did not translate to a consistent whole-command improvement. No direct native speedup follows from this comparison.

The mapped-symbol resolution audit assigns each sample to the outermost getTypeOfMappedSymbol source line on its stack so recursion cannot double-count CPU. The resolver accounted for 0.64% of retained TypeORM CPU, 0.17% on tRPC, 0.38% on VS Code and 3.03% on Sentry. On the size-aware Sentry profile it accounted for 5.03 seconds (4.23%). Of those, 3.12 seconds were under the template-instantiation call, 1.10 under the value-link lookup and 0.57 at the resolvedType nil test. That test also executes on misses. Existing four-checker event counts recorded 14,369,061 hits and 452,497 misses on Sentry, so event frequency alone is misleading as a CPU budget. The next observer distinguishes repeated, completed instantiation inputs within a checker before considering reuse.

On the size-aware Sentry build, 18.78% of sampled CPU had instantiateType, instantiateTypeWithAlias or instantiateTypeWorker on stack. Attributing each sample to the caller of the outermost such frame, type alias instantiation accounted for 7.58 CPU seconds, mapped index member construction 4.95, mapped symbol resolution 2.92 and instantiated symbols 2.48. These figures include callees; a narrower nested call may have its work attributed to an outer caller.

The mapped instantiation observer (overlay on 395df1a plus size-aware heap advice) records exact checker-local pointer tuples for template, outer mapper, mapped parameter and key. It checks whether the tuple already had a completed result at entry, then compares the real returned pointer. All original instantiation calls still run. All six extended diagnostic work counters matched the uninstrumented build on each pinned project. A generic property fixture with two TS2322 diagnostics matched pinned upstream Go and the size-aware Go build. No completed tuples repeated across 7,564 TypeORM calls, 3,479 tRPC calls or 98,532 VS Code calls. Sentry repeated 1,351 of 452,497 calls (0.30%); all returned the same pointer as the first completion. This counter does not equate different outer mapper identities, measure the cost of the repeat subset or establish the safety of bypassing diagnostics and recursion limits. A checker-local cache keyed by these exact inputs would retain 451,146 descriptors on Sentry for very few hits. Do not add it to production.

Of 7.58 sampled CPU seconds under the outer type-alias cache-miss instantiation call in the size-aware Sentry profile, 5.98 entered the conditional-type worker branch and 1.43 entered the object branch. The conditional path spent 4.39 seconds beneath direct conditional evaluation, 1.12 beneath union distribution and 0.43 reducing the distributive input. These disjoint stack groups include callees. The direct evaluation group alone accounts for 3.69% of total sampled CPU. Parallelizing the distribution loop alone would touch less than 1% of total sampled CPU in this calling context. The profile analysis now reads the matching historical checker source for its line labels.

Across every calling context, the outermost conditional evaluation accounted for 9.29 sampled CPU seconds on Sentry. Instantiating the selected true branch accounted for 5.71 seconds (61.5% of that group), the permissive assignability test and its operands 1.32 seconds, and inference 0.85 seconds. This tally overlaps the alias-miss groups. The costly path constructs the resulting type after branch selection, so accelerating the predicate alone would leave most of this work in place.

Within the selected true branch, 5.30 of 5.71 sampled seconds entered the union/intersection worker. Of those, 5.17 seconds fell beneath substitution of the constituent list, 0.09 beneath union normalization and 0.04 beneath intersection normalization. This points to reducing recursive work per constituent. SIMD over the list or a faster final union table would still leave the recursive substitutions. These groups come from one Sentry profile and include nested operations.

The generic-arity change removes seven calls that walk type parameters solely to compute an unused minimum for fillMissingTypeArguments. The exported checker method keeps its signature for language-service callers. Where arity is actually checked, scanning backwards stops at the rightmost required parameter, including when an invalid declaration has a required parameter after a default. This introduces no shared state or new cache. Go checker and compiler package tests passed. All 85 check/emit comparisons matched native tsc-ts (including diagnostic text and emitted bytes); all five new generic-arity variants also matched pinned upstream Go. Both large pinned no-emit projects completed without diagnostics. Historical raw CPU profiles attributed at most 0.086% of samples on each project to getMinTypeArgumentCount or hasTypeParameterDefault; the change is expected to remove work below the precision of short end-to-end comparisons. An initial small-project timing screen overlapped large-project validation and was discarded. The replacement screen runs alone with the same compiler flags, six measured pairs, alternating order, and one excluded warmup.

The isolated screen gave control/candidate medians of 2596.40/2593.93 ms on TypeORM and 537.93/530.71 ms on tRPC. Ratios of medians were 1.001× and 1.014× while medians of the paired ratios were 0.989× and 0.990×. This screen resolves no consistent wall-time improvement. Keep the simpler checker implementation for the eliminated declaration walks. The native 10× target remains open.

Continuing the size-aware Sentry profile from 395df1a, 4.87 of the 5.17 sampled CPU seconds beneath constituent substitution entered another conditional type. Direct evaluation accounted for 4.75 seconds. Within that evaluation, substituting the extends operand accounted for 2.63 seconds and inference for 1.18. Of the 2.63, instantiating a keyof expression accounted for 1.69 seconds and indexed access for 0.68.
