Inside the keyof group, 0.86 seconds followed the intersection branch and 0.80 went through property-to-literal collection. These nested totals include callees and cannot be added. This identifies two testable mechanisms: bulk construction of literal key unions and checker-local repetition of keyof on intersections.

Bulk literal-union construction now detects an empty output set and at least 16 plain, unaliased string literal inputs owned by the same checker. It copies the input, sorts with the existing CompareTypes ordering, compacts duplicate pointers, and preserves the accumulated include flags. For these types the comparator breaks equal string values by the unique checker-local type ID, so fresh and regular literals remain distinct. Large reverse-ordered inputs avoid the quadratic movement from inserting each pointer at the front. Mixed types, enum literals and aliased literals retain their existing insertion path.

Checker and compiler package tests passed, including differential tests against per-element ordered insertion at 15 and 16 entries and on fresh, regular and duplicate literals. All 90 check and emit comparisons matched native tsc-ts. The new five variants also matched upstream Go. In the isolated three-compiler scaling screen, 131,072 descending keys took 2280.61 ms native, 2276.06 ms prior Go and 367.06 ms candidate Go. With 32,768 keys, descending literal inputs improved 1.70× versus prior Go; a permuted keyof object improved 1.05×. Each compiler ran once per position across three measured blocks after one warmup; all rejected an invalid key with identical diagnostics and accepted the timed input.

On the pinned projects, the candidate/control timing screen was mixed. The reference/candidate ratios of medians were 1.016× TypeORM, 0.850× tRPC, 0.848× VS Code and 1.016× Sentry. Each large project favored a different build in its two pairs. A separate 12-pair tRPC run reversed the binary slots and gave six wins per compiler and a paired median ratio of 0.992×. Keep the algorithm for its reproducible scaling improvement on real compiler inputs. The four project measurements establish no overall acceleration and cannot be used to claim the native 10× goal.

A checker-local observer counted exact (reduced intersection pointer, index flags) requests at the intersection branch of getIndexTypeEx. Each invocation still ran the full operation. Completed repeats / total calls were 86/159 TypeORM, 23/190 tRPC, 313/5,069 VS Code and 3,758/9,219 Sentry. Every repeat returned its original result pointer, with zero active re-entries. However, 16 Sentry repeats increased the checker's total instantiation count. Skipping all completed repeats would suppress observed semantic work, which matters for instantiation limits. No result cache was added. Results do not measure time saved or prove that the other repeats are effect-free. The focused fixture observed one repeat of two calls and matched the expected error in both builds.

All four full projects had clean diagnostics. The observer matched all six extended work counters on three projects. TypeORM differed only in Symbols (461,595 vs 461,597); three additional control-only runs produced 461,595, 461,597 and 461,597. The normal local package was rebuilt at 7367ef4 with its source provenance. Its normal CLI reports 7.0.2 and the five new check/emit cases passed against native tsc-ts.

The split keyof-intersection observer ran all original constituent queries and union construction. Sentry had identical ordered operand pointers on all 3,758 completed repeats. All 16 repeats that increased TotalInstantiationCount did so during operand resolution; zero did so during final union construction. Of the repeats, 1,693 were already eligible for the existing two-type union cache. Of the other 2,065, only 37 had two operands. TypeORM, tRPC and VS Code respectively had 42, 6 and 57 repeats outside the existing pair-cache shape. An identical tuple and result do not prove absence of every semantic effect. The observer was diagnostic only. All four projects checked cleanly. Three projects matched all six extended work counters. TypeORM differed only in Symbols (461,599 control vs 461,593 observer; additional controls 461,595, 461,597, 461,595).

Across all stacks in the retained size-aware Sentry profile, the outermost intersection branch of getIndexTypeEx accounted for 1.41 sampled CPU seconds: 0.34 beneath operand resolution and 1.07 beneath final union construction. Within that construction, 0.21 seconds went through a two-type union cache miss and 0.86 through the general worker. This is a narrow opportunity on a 119.04 CPU-second profile. General union construction is a broader target; the outermost observed getUnionTypeWorker accounted for 1.23% TypeORM, 0.68% tRPC, 3.09% VS Code and 5.73% Sentry on the historical four-project profiles. On Sentry, addTypesToUnion accounted for 5.94 sampled seconds directly beneath the worker; on VS Code, removeSubtypes dominated at 8.52 seconds. Stack truncation can omit outer calls, and CPU shares are not forecasts of wall-clock speedup.

Breaking down the historical VS Code removeSubtypes samples directly under getUnionTypeWorker, 3.78 CPU seconds were attributed to core.Find (the first-unit-property scan), 2.87 to obtaining the source properties, and the rest to other work. On Sentry the corresponding samples totaled 1.58 and 0.86 seconds. A diagnostic build based on a261586 observes source type pointers, returned property and regular literal type, visited property counts, and changes in TotalInstantiationCount while preserving the original searches and target comparisons. The focused three-object fixture matched the upstream and Go control diagnostic. It observed seven searches, four repeated sources and ten target comparisons skipped via the existing discriminant test.

The completed subtype-key search observer found repeated source types accounted for 4,347/18,240 property visits on TypeORM, 484/3,081 on tRPC, 292,564/921,227 on VS Code and 311,247/644,987 on Sentry. All 205, 128, 13,924 and 17,486 respective repeated searches returned the same property and regular literal type pointers as the first completed observation. None increased TotalInstantiationCount on a repeat, but first searches can instantiate types (9,772 searches on VS Code, 5,334 on Sentry). These counts do not establish that skipping the repeated searches preserves diagnostics, lazy symbol state or other effects. All project diagnostics were clean; all six extended work counters matched on three workloads, and only Symbols differed on TypeORM (461,599 retained control, 461,595 observer). Controls have previously produced the latter count as well.

Sorted-run union merging takes a known nested union with at least 32 constituents and an existing set of at least 32 types. It checks that the incoming members are strictly increasing plain, unaliased string literals in this checker, locates the affected range using CompareTypes, and performs one insertion or merges the overlap. Named union flags and origin processing remain in their existing callers. The merged output uses the compiler's type ordering, including its distinction between fresh and regular literal identities. The four-project timing control was the previous bulk-literal Go build, with matching embedded Go build metadata. The full checker and compiler package tests passed; the finalized differential test also passed separately. All 95 native check/emit parity cases passed and the five new cases matched upstream Go.

On Sentry 56,949 eligible runs covered 4,076,380 incoming elements. The existing set could append the whole run in 56,047 cases; 869 required an overlap merge. TypeORM reached the path five times and VS Code 35 times. tRPC had zero eligible runs. The pinned timing ratios were 0.970× TypeORM, 1.011× tRPC, 0.999× VS Code and 1.018× Sentry. Both Sentry pairs favored the candidate (1.025× and 1.010×). In the generated 131,072-key interleaved case the prior Go build took 671.61 ms, native tsc-ts 544.84 ms and the merge build 382.65 ms. Keep the change for the measured large-union scaling and frequent real Sentry operations. The four-project native 10× target remains open.

A separate cold frontend investigation labels fileLoader.parseSourceFile around its ordinary GetSourceFile call in the a261586 compiler. It profiles fresh --listFilesOnly processes, verifies the discovered files against the frozen inputs, and records only CPU spent building the program, with no checking or emit. Three profiles each on TypeORM and tRPC and one each on VS Code and Sentry were archived; all eight source-loading totals were independently reproduced with pprof -tags. Per-file label setup and unrelated program-building activity are outside the labelled samples. Input bytes were independently counted after verifying every SHA-256.

Of the CPU samples within labelled source loading, project/workspace non-declaration inputs accounted for 83.2% TypeORM, 12.1% tRPC, 96.2% VS Code and 79.2% Sentry. Node_modules declarations accounted for 15.6%, 72.5%, 2.8% and 18.2%; the compiler's own libraries accounted for 1.2%, 15.4%, 0.5% and 2.1%. Declaration loading is an attractive tRPC-specific target. A frontend improvement across all four must also accelerate ordinary project loading and parsing. These are instrumented CPU distributions within GetSourceFile; they do not predict end-to-end wall time. The local normal compiler package was rebuilt at 7a4ec65 and its five new named-union parity cases passed.

An ideal-parallelism model from the balanced native/size-aware-Go cold checks at the heap-page stage uses per-process user plus system CPU, with block zero excluded. Dividing median Go CPU seconds by one tenth of median native wall time gives 82.5 ideal cores for TypeORM, 67.5 for tRPC, 68.0 for VS Code and 80.6 for Sentry if aggregate CPU work stays fixed. At five fully occupied cores the required reductions in total Go CPU work are 16.5×, 13.5×, 13.6× and 16.1× respectively. Even an ideal eight-core run requires 10.3×, 8.4×, 8.5× and 10.1×. This model assumes perfect parallelism and constant per-core processing rate. It is an optimistic bound for scheduling unchanged work on those inputs, not a measurement of a many-core host. It reinforces the need to reduce work across frontend and semantic phases.

The live symbol-slot experiment gives each checker a 1,024-entry table of candidate lookup positions. It verifies the live entry's key and reads the current type, including after entry replacement. The matched Go control and candidate had clean diagnostics on all four projects. TypeORM split the six pairs, tRPC favored the candidate in six, VS Code split its two pairs, and Sentry favored the control in both by approximately 3.5%. The raw samples and staged patch are in benchmarks/profiles/live-symbol-slots. The production default is still the sorted-literal-run build. Focused stale-link, dispatch, concurrency and pointer checks passed, as did the checker/compiler/core package tests, 100 native check/emit comparisons across the existing and new fixtures, the new five-mode fixture against pinned upstream Go, and all 12 native API/emit integration tests.

The symbol lookup shadow ran the original resolver at all 52,354,995 would-be returns across the four pinned checks. It observed zero differences in returned type identity and zero changes to checker type, symbol and instantiation counts across those individual calls. Whole-project counters agreed except for the previously variable TypeORM Symbols count (461,595 control, 461,597 shadow). In the isolated 12-pair tRPC confirmation with roles reversed, the control was 486.60 ms and the candidate 493.55 ms. The initial improvement did not reproduce. The earlier reverse run overlapped two Go builds and is archived separately as trpc-build-overlap.json.gz. Keep the candidate as a patch only.

A cold declaration inventory at 7d69a8d traversed the declaration SourceFile objects returned by the loader, then recorded distinct type nodes actually passed to getTypeFromTypeNodeWorker on the full checks. The requested fractions were 10.6% of 70,121 on TypeORM, 6.4% of 169,949 on tRPC, 26.4% of 251,145 on VS Code and 21.6% of 480,521 on Sentry. Disjoint type-node spans with no worker request in their subtree covered 16.5%, 21.3%, 17.8% and 23.1% of parsed declaration text respectively, including leading trivia. This inventory counts loaded declaration objects, which may differ from unique final program paths. Other checker entry points, the binder, syntactic diagnostics, emit and API clients can still require these nodes. These are opportunity measurements for deferred construction; a parser still needs to find boundaries and maintain diagnostics. All six extended work counters matched the control on three projects; TypeORM differed only in its variable Symbols count (461,595 versus 461,599).

Template-pattern union reduction formerly deleted each matched string literal in reverse order, moving the surviving suffix once per match. Alternating matches can therefore require quadratic pointer movement. The first prototype packed survivors from the right in the original evaluation order and copied them back. Its generated 131,072-literal screening medians were 835.57 ms for the prior Go compiler and 626.95 ms for the prototype. Its screen overlapped package-test compilation. On Sentry the original algorithm shifted 7,354 pointers across 67 matches; the prototype would write 17,122 surviving pointers. VS Code had 95 matches, and TypeORM and tRPC had zero. The prototype is recorded as a draft, with its own source overlay and samples.

The final implementation marks matched members of the privately constructed union with nil and performs one stable compaction from the first hole. The matcher is still called in the same reverse order with the original pattern list. Pattern types are disjoint from the removed string literals. Each affected survivor is copied at most once, the prefix stays in place, and the discarded tail is cleared. All 256 placements of matches among eight ordered keys passed the checker test for pointer identity, order and cleared tail slots. The checker and compiler package tests also passed.

The clean packaged 50291d2 compiler checked 262,144 alternating literals in 1,124.98 ms versus 3,049.76 ms for the previous shipped Go build and 3,326.48 ms for original native tsc-ts. On 131,072 keys the same ratios were 1.705× and 1.631×. At 4,096 keys, 12 balanced confirmation blocks gave 32.36 ms new Go versus 30.40 ms prior Go and 22.73 ms native. These generated full checks include process startup and validate accepted and rejected assignments. The change addresses worst-case movement; the real workload counts show limited demand. All 105 native CLI parity variants passed on the final implementation, and the focused five-mode upstream Go regression passed again through the clean package.

Two alternating fresh-process pairs per project (plus an excluded warmup) checked the shipped executable directly against original native tsc-ts. Native/Go ratios were 0.556× VS Code, 0.512× Sentry, 0.487× TypeORM and 0.689× tRPC, with matching frozen inputs and clean diagnostics throughout. The shipped Go remains about 14.5–20.5× away from the tenfold native target. Sufficiently broad improvements must reduce frontend and semantic work together. The declaration type-node demand figures identify a possible frontend target, especially for tRPC, but cannot predict recoverable time or justify skipping validation and binding.

The per-file parser slab census counts each call that allocates one element from the AST NodeFactory's 48 typed arenas during normal list-files discovery. It observed all frozen final inputs exactly once, plus 22 extra parses on VS Code, five on Sentry, two on TypeORM and three on tRPC. The model uses capacities from slices.Grow on the pinned Go toolchain, including the rounded capacity used to size the following page. It counts backing allocations and element capacity, excluding object headers, allocator rounding beyond whole elements, other parser allocations and post-release retention.

A weighted slab for the first allocations of 16 common types on files of at least 1,024 bytes models 19.7% fewer typed arena backings on VS Code, 24.5% on Sentry, 21.0% on TypeORM and 17.2% on tRPC. Corresponding modeled payload capacity increases are 0.38%, 1.46%, 1.08% and 1.65%. The candidate puts the arrays in one concrete Go struct with typed GC layout; subsequent per-type growth uses the existing arena and no pointers move. An escaped node can retain siblings in the same initial slab. The model covers all inventoried typed arenas and does not predict whole-process allocation or timing changes.

Across the same parses, unused slots occupied 21.1% of VS Code's modeled typed arena capacity, 26.8% on Sentry and TypeORM, and 20.9% on tRPC. Identifier had the largest unused payload on all four. A separate model keeps allocation lazy, choosing a first page and a growth cap per type. It permits a change only if both allocation count and payload capacity are no higher on every training project for that type. With each project excluded from training in turn, predicted allocations and capacity both fell on the excluded project. Those held-out reductions ranged from 3.3% to 19.0% of typed backing allocations and 0.8% to 6.9% of their payload bytes. A candidate fitted on all four projects changes 12 type-specific allocation sites for the AST factory, including non-parser clients; the model only covers the per-file parser. Actual full checking, emit and timing need separate validation.

The shared-first-page compiler passed the AST, parser and compiler suites and 105 native CLI parity tests. Its measured discovery runs produced fewer process allocations on all projects. Counts fell by 31,094 TypeORM, 7,129 tRPC, 213,198 VS Code and 139,657 Sentry. The reported post-GC memory rose by 2,114, 1,323, 11,115 and 18,122 KiB respectively. Four-pair standalone discovery ratios were 1.032×, 0.959×, 0.988× and 0.968×. Full-check ratios against the matched Go control were 1.072×, 0.959×, 1.004× and 1.021× respectively. TypeORM's first pair was 1.491×, while three of four tRPC pairs favored the control. Keep the shared-slab candidate experimental given the higher memory and mixed full-project behavior.

The lazy per-kind candidate passed core, AST, parser and compiler package tests, including stable element identity after page growth and garbage collection. All 105 CLI check and emit comparisons matched original native tsc-ts. Against pinned upstream Go, 102/105 matched; three declaration-emitting variants of “mapped union declaration order across checker histories” placed the same two union members in opposite order. The matched Go control reproduced those same three upstream differences, and candidate versus control agreed in all five modes for that fixture. This matches the maintained fork’s documented deterministic union ordering. The native and upstream reports are archived separately.

Compared with its matched Go control, lazy page growth reduced post-GC discovery/bind memory by 1,672 KiB on TypeORM, 1,228 KiB on tRPC, 91,602 KiB on VS Code and 22,931 KiB on Sentry. The six compiler work counters agreed in these runs. Four-pair discovery ratios were 1.066×, 0.949×, 0.992× and 1.016× respectively (see PERF.md for rounded timings). Initial full-check ratios were 1.038× TypeORM, 1.077× tRPC, 1.024× VS Code and 0.939× Sentry. Both VS Code pairs favored the candidate; Sentry split 0.866× and 1.011×. The reversed-order small-project confirmation did not reproduce the early gains.

The lazy-growth confirmation reversed the binary roles and retained identical project inputs and compiler flags. TypeORM's control/candidate median ratio was 0.993× (candidate faster in 3/8 pairs), tRPC's 0.928× (0/8) and Sentry's 0.962× (1/4). The 12 API, project and parallel-emit integration tests also passed. Keep the candidate experimental; its lower post-GC discovery memory did not translate into reproducible full-check acceleration. The shipping compiler remains 50291d2 and the cold native 10× target remains open.

A separate audit of the archived full-check heap profiles counted samples whose leaf was a generated NodeFactory constructor that directly allocates a concrete syntax object outside the typed arenas. This accounted for an estimated 5.47% of TypeORM, 10.89% of tRPC, 13.08% of VS Code and 6.43% of Sentry allocation objects. Corresponding sampled allocation-byte shares were 2.89%, 3.44%, 4.21% and 2.79%. ObjectLiteralExpression dominated these counts on VS Code and Sentry; import, array literal and JSX constructors also appeared among their largest groups. These are weighted, historical allocation samples over all factory consumers. They identify which constructors to instrument next; they do not establish an avoidable time fraction.

A direct-node inventory traversed the ordinary AST child links of every file in the four frozen input lists during file discovery. It counts surviving syntax kinds with a statically identifiable concrete, directly allocated generated constructor. It does not count discarded parser speculation or force lazy JSDoc. The most frequent such node on VS Code and Sentry was ObjectLiteralExpression (321,234 and 140,489 respectively), while ArrowFunction led on TypeORM (15,186) and QualifiedName on tRPC (7,397). On TypeORM and tRPC, giving each file's import clauses a standalone first node and subsequent pages of eight would leave 13,012 and 1,830 unused slots alongside 12,638 and 1,960 live clauses. This motivated a structural experiment that allocates the clause and enclosing import declaration together only when both are parsed. Each node still runs through its original initialization, factory accounting, hooks and parser finishing stage. Side-effect imports, import-equals and JSDoc import tags retain separate construction.

The paired import-node build passed the AST, parser and compiler packages (including escaped clause identity after GC) and all 105 native CLI parity checks. Separate discovery/bind diagnostic processes reported 5,196 fewer allocation events on tRPC, 3,229 on TypeORM, 129,384 on VS Code and 91,818 on Sentry. These process totals include concurrent runtime activity. A discovery-only run on VS Code took a median of 5.651 s in the matched control and 5.649 s in the candidate. The lower allocation count alone does not justify adoption.

On 1,000 named imports, the paired allocation removed exactly 1,000 median allocations per parse (3,108 to 2,108). The six-block median wall time rose from 1.972 to 2.051 ms. The parser-only test uses ordinary Go test compilation. Full cold checks with the matched optimized CLIs produced control/candidate ratios of 1.048× TypeORM, 0.981× tRPC, 0.952× VS Code and 1.049× Sentry. The candidate and control also agreed on diagnostics, failure status and all six emitted files for valid and malformed imports, including JSDoc imports. Keep coallocation experimental because its lower allocation count did not translate into consistent cold-check gains.

The primitive union intersection worker inserts each newly encountered member into a temporary sorted slice, then checks membership in every input. With two interleaved pure string literal sets this can shift a quadratic number of pointers. Two cursors suffice because the input unions already use CompareTypes order and a plain string literal can only match by exact membership in another plain literal set. The fast path requires two operands and at least 64 combined members. All other types, including wide primitives, enum literals, undefined/missing and intersections of three or more union operands, retain the existing matching rules. A counter-only control found 49 eligible intersections (22,337 incoming members) on Sentry, two (266 members) on VS Code and zero on TypeORM and tRPC. The generated mixed/disjoint/contained/singleton and negative cases agree with the native compiler. Initial standalone full-check scaling with ordinary Go build settings reached 435.49 ms control and 227.77 ms candidate at a key range of 131,072. The sizes label the generated key range; the two input sets use multiples of two and three.

Shipping compiler e1eeaab passed checker and compiler package tests, all 110 native CLI comparisons, and the independent intersection set oracle. Three order-balanced generated full checks on a 524,288-key range had medians of 18,937 ms for the prior optimized Go control, 4,651 ms for original native and 867 ms for the shipped build (21.83× versus Go and 5.36× versus native). At a 983,040-key range the separate ordinary-build prototype ran in 2,519 ms against 101,819 ms ordinary Go and 24,167 ms native. The real cold workload run compared the shipped executable directly against native in two alternating pairs per project, after an excluded warmup. Native/Go ratios were 0.525× VS Code, 0.495× Sentry, 0.543× TypeORM and 0.660× tRPC. Exact project revisions, input identities, command lines and clean diagnostics were verified. The generated scaling improvement reaches real checker calls on two projects. It does not achieve the requested 10× across real cold checks.

Fresh full cold-check CPU profiles of shipping compiler e1eeaab put pointer-link table operations on 4.91% of sampled CPU on tRPC, 4.59% on TypeORM, 8.27% on VS Code and 6.41% on Sentry. Go map functions were on 17.19%, 11.59%, 18.15% and 24.88% respectively, and concurrent marking / assist functions on 18.60%, 17.74%, 16.92% and 15.04%. These stack families can overlap. They include descendants, and GC workers run concurrently. They do not translate directly to removable wall time. On VS Code the largest two leaf source lines in PointerLinkStore accounted for 9.71 CPU seconds checking an occupied key or empty slot and 3.28 seconds placing an entry while growing the table. The same lines on Sentry accounted for 7.00 and 2.89 CPU seconds. Each large project profile contains over 200 seconds of sampled CPU. The short tRPC profile contains 2.85 seconds. Archives and exact input/binary identities are in benchmarks/profiles/shipped-cpu-costs.

The source attribution for ordinary Go maps assigns each sample containing a map frame to the nearest compiler caller above the innermost map frame. On Sentry the relation-result read accounted for 4.46% of all CPU samples and the relation write 0.94%. The same read was 1.09% on VS Code. Generic LinkStore.Get was the highest such source line on VS Code (2.34%). These are call-path costs in one independently profiled full check per project. CPU samples attributed to Go maps and pointer tables are separate analyses.

The pointer table experiment compares the existing consecutive probe sequence against triangular offsets 0, 1, 3, 6, ... with the same hash, 75% growth threshold, key/value entry layout and arena. Because table capacity is a power of two the triangular offsets also visit every index before the bound. The observer samples non-nil keys whose mixed hash has seven zero high bits. Counts are weighted by accesses to sampled keys, not by drawing independent random samples per operation. It records probe invocations ending on a matching key or an empty slot and counts grow-time reinsertion separately. Empty-slot scans can include an insertion's second search after growth. Observed counts are diagnostic only. The uninstrumented candidate is measured separately.

Historical PERF rows labelled Native tsc-ts or original native used the locally built upstream source executable (hash 525c0fe9…, compiler version 7.0.2). Its binary differs from the official npm 0.0.2 Linux GNU binary (fb24c9cc…, also compiler 7.0.2). The public package tagged latest when verified on 2026-11-21 is tsc-ts@0.1.5 (compiler version 7.1.0-beta, released November 11, binary 40164176…). The historical rows are source-build comparisons. Release tarball integrity values, launcher hashes and binary identities are archived in benchmarks/profiles/public-npm/releases.json. Public CLI measurements use the npm launcher, including its Node process startup, and the release's bundled declaration files for both compilers.

Two instrumented fresh runs per project found mean empty-slot probes of 3.14–3.52 with linear probing and 2.47–2.64 with triangular probing. Both values count the final empty slot. The clean triangular candidate passed the pointer store unit tests and all 110 native CLI comparisons. With matched ordinary Go builds and no counters its project medians were slower across all four (control/candidate 0.849× VS Code, 0.983× Sentry, 0.967× TypeORM and 0.970× tRPC). Reducing probes by spreading them farther apart did not produce an end-to-end win. Keep the existing production pointer table.

Direct public npm 0.1.5 launcher comparisons on the pinned projects used release libraries on both sides. Two alternating pairs after one excluded warmup gave 416.67 ms public versus 822.42 ms Go for tRPC, and 1,422.38 ms versus 4,005.86 ms for TypeORM. Project revisions, compiler arguments, input contents and clean diagnostic output were verified. The new public compiler exited 2 on the clean VS Code and Sentry workloads, reporting 10 diagnostics and two diagnostics respectively. Keep those reports as failed preflights; no clean timing ratio can be assigned. The historic local compiler's earlier clean outputs do not establish that the latest release is equivalent on these projects.

The published npm 0.0.2 launcher (compiler 7.0.2) completed all four checks cleanly. After normalizing the absolute library installation directory, every input name and SHA-256 matched the historic workloads. In two alternating pairs the public-release / Go medians in seconds were 31.874 / 48.692 VS Code, 14.182 / 27.664 Sentry, 1.449 / 2.602 TypeORM and 0.397 / 0.466 tRPC. Compare timings within each paired campaign; the public 0.0.2 and 0.1.5 campaigns are separate.

A two-by-two diagnostic check independently varied the public 0.1.5 and shipped Go compiler and the original and published standard-library directories. VS Code's public compiler emitted ten diagnostics with either library set while Go checked cleanly with either on matching inputs. For Sentry, the public compiler reported one diagnostic with original libraries and two with release libraries. Go checked cleanly with the original libraries but reported 897 diagnostics with release libraries; the discovered files also differ between the engines. Do not use those Sentry checks for a speed comparison. The archived matrix retains actual exit codes, diagnostics and file differences from the frozen full workload.

Union normalization appears on 0.70% of tRPC, 1.75% of TypeORM, 3.38% of VS Code and 6.06% of Sentry sampled production CPU stacks. A diagnostic probe models 4,096 slots per checker on the unhandled ordered lists of two to four non-nil inputs with nil origin and alias, after the existing union-pair cache. It recomputes every request. Of the two-input requests in the pure literal/intrinsic group, 345,993 of 364,874 in VS Code and 125,988 of 141,083 in Sentry matched a resident entry. Across both short-input groups and all four workloads no observed simulated hit changed type identity or increased the instantiation counter. This evidence does not cover diagnostics, flow, side effects other than instantiation, or unobserved programs. The ordinary symbol resolver's widening and initializer path has a larger CPU budget across the projects and is the first optimization target.

Ranked the checker hot paths by inclusive and exclusive contexts from the shipped full profiles. Outermost overload selection inside declaration initializers consumes 6.42% of VS Code and 9.51% of Sentry CPU; first generic argument inference dominates the individual source sites at 3.57% and 4.01%. The cheaper quick/fallback callee-reuse hypothesis was checked on all four frozen projects. Of 65,202 repeated callee checks in VS Code, 34 produced a different raw type identity and 38 performed instantiation work. On Sentry the corresponding counts were 24,600, eight and eight. These are independent observations and their subsets may overlap. No result substitution is sound from AST identity alone. Further work should start at inference and signature applicability across overload candidates. Detailed ranked sites and scope are in benchmarks/profiles/shipped-cpu-costs/PROFILE.md.

In the shipped CPU profiles, the outermost inferTypeArguments invocation inside an initializer most often spends time checking an argument expression against its contextual parameter type (checker.go:9464). This source line accounts for 3.42% of all VS Code CPU, 3.86% of Sentry, 1.45% of TypeORM and 2.11% of tRPC. The immediately following inferTypes constraint update accounts for 0.36%, 0.63%, 0.54% and 0.35% respectively. Inside the argument checks, nested call expression handlers lead VS Code at 2.32% of total CPU. Function expressions and object literal methods lead Sentry at 1.68%, followed by object literal expressions at 0.99% and array literals at 0.70%. These inclusive source-line budgets include work in callees.

A call-local observer in resolveCall rechecked every argument normally. It tracked the previous result, signature, check mode and inference-context pointer by exact argument node and parameter type, with independent frames for nested calls. It covers ordinary positional argument checks in inferTypeArguments and isSignatureApplicable, excluding JSX-specific, rest-spread and this-argument paths. Inference repeats across different signatures were rare: 157/363,683 VS Code, 239/143,578 Sentry, 29/31,328 TypeORM and 1/1,467 tRPC. Within inference no repeat had identical mode and context. Even the stricter applicability repeats returned changed type identities in 1,494 cases on VS Code, 141 on Sentry, 40 on TypeORM and six on tRPC. An inference-context pointer is not a snapshot of its mutable state. No compiler time saved is claimed. Prioritize first-pass nested-call resolution on VS Code and function/object argument construction on Sentry.

Two independent experiments follow the workload profiles. The signature observer classifies getResolvedSignature requests while executing contextual argument checks during declaration initializer inference. It retains per-checker node history and active resolution depth to separate first work, recursive reentry, candidate-list requests, previously deferred markers and other repeat misses. Both normal and targeted lookups execute their original path. A separate scalar-pair union candidate uses a lazily allocated 1,024-entry checker-local table with exact ordered pointer comparisons. It covers only alias-free, origin-free, literal-reduction pairs of plain intrinsic and literal types. Unequal keys overwrite one slot; candidate results still originate in the original union worker. A dedicated differential test exercises repeated hits, reversed pairs, widening to a base primitive and collisions with 2,100 distinct literals. It passed against the exact original worker result identities.

With per-checker resolution history, call expressions underneath initializer argument inference had 42,573 signature misses on VS Code and 16,005 on Sentry. First resolutions accounted for 41,679 (97.9%) and 15,906 (99.4%) respectively. VS Code had 884 repeat deferred-marker misses, four active reentries and six other repeat misses. Sentry had 82 deferred-marker misses and 17 active reentries. TypeORM had 745 first resolutions and no repeats in this scope. tRPC had 218 first resolutions and 38 deferred-marker repeats. Counts are invocations rather than CPU weights. Focus on the cost of first-time resolution and localize expensive source inputs. Increasing signature cache size cannot remove those first visits.

The scalar-pair candidate passed 110 native comparisons but regressed median cold-check time on TypeORM by 12.6% and Sentry by 9.7%. Median times fell 2.5% on VS Code and 0.9% on tRPC. With two pairs per large project and four per small project, these are screening measurements. The cache stays experimental. PERF.md links the matched Go samples for each workload.

File-labelled Go CPU profiles from clean checks of all four pinned projects are in benchmarks/profiles/source-file-cpu. Labels identify the triggering file, including work on its dependencies. The five busiest files accounted for 8.6% of labelled CPU on VS Code, 7.8% on Sentry, 5.8% on TypeORM and 28.4% on tRPC (only 67 labelled samples). Focus experiments on compiler operations that recur across files.

On VS Code, mapSessionEvents.test.ts triggered 1.84 CPU seconds, of which 1.76 included inferTypeArguments and 1.33 included inferToMultipleTypes. Inspect the nested source-by-target walk in inference.go with this file as a test case. Measure which pairs return before updating inference candidates, priority or circularity. In the unmodified shipping build the function was on 2.30% of all VS Code CPU stacks and 3.87% of Sentry stacks. On Sentry, starredSegmentCell.tsx triggered 2.43 CPU seconds, of which 2.17 included isApplicableIndexType. This function was on 7.37% of all Sentry CPU stacks in the shipping profile. Measure the index-key types involved in repeated assignability comparisons originating from this file. These are inclusive samples from separate diagnostic and shipping runs. File-local percentages do not predict project speedup.

The TypeScript checker already rejects incompatible fixed ends in isApplicableIndexType, for string literals and template keys whose source placeholders are string, number or bigint. Ported that rule to the Go index lookup. Other placeholder forms still enter assignability. This intentionally avoids creating relation entries for disjoint keys, as the repository's TypeScript implementation does. The checker, compiler and CLI unit suites passed. The original public tsc-ts@0.0.2 and the Go candidate agreed on diagnostics, exit status and emitted bytes in all five variants of the new overlapping/generic index fixture. Full workload timings follow independently.

A separate prototype reads an existing completed template-pair relation entry before entering the relater, including its reliability flags. It passed focused checks against the full assignability path, including overflow fallback. It was measured on a single resident pair during an unrelated build, so those microbenchmark times do not establish a production speedup. The prefix/suffix rule can avoid even a first-time comparison and was selected for the workload screen.

Shipping build a6444de passed all 115 native differential checks. The candidate with a range index is isolated in benchmarks/profiles/template-index-ranges. For wide lists of exclusively template indexes it hashes a common number of leading bytes, then scans only the interval containing the matching prefix group. It reads the current entries in their original order, including multiple matches and any replaced value for an existing key. Small, mixed, empty-prefix and nonconcrete cases retain the ordinary scan. Focused collector tests check exhaustive ordered coverage and same-key value replacement. Its performance and full compatibility remain under investigation.

The matched shipping-build screen used the same Go toolchain, project inlining flags, input file hashes and clean diagnostics. With two pairs, Sentry improved 33.70 s to 30.90 s and its median peak RSS fell 5,313 to 4,936 MiB. VS Code, TypeORM and tRPC were slower in both pairs, with ratios of medians 0.963x, 0.892x and 0.880x. The range prototype build overlapped early VS Code preflight. GNU objdump shows findApplicableIndexInfo called isTypeRelatedTo directly in the previous build; the new build calls isApplicableIndexType out of line. This establishes an inlining change, not its contribution to wall time. Recheck the small projects and test retaining the old ordinary-key fast path.

Five rotating fresh-process measurements per compiler on the generated 1,536-signature project put the shipping guard at 376.52 ms versus 6,873.92 ms for the preceding shipping Go build and 474.47 ms for the original public npm release. The checker read and accepted the same source throughout. The range index was a further 1.112x over the guard in matched ordinary builds (363.14 to 326.53 ms). The fixture exercises many independent index keys. Its speedup is specific to this pattern and the 10x goal for the four real projects remains open.

Direct, alternating timings against the installed original npm tsc-ts@0.0.2 used identical input bytes, clean output and the same four pinned revisions. Public/Go median ratios were 0.595x VS Code, 0.630x Sentry, 0.524x TypeORM and 0.808x tRPC (two pairs each). The public release remains faster on every target project. This is a separate screen from the matched Go comparison.

On the follow-up pinned Sentry run, isApplicableIndexType fell from 7.37% in the old shipping CPU profile to 0.50% in the new one; findApplicableIndexInfo fell from 6.77% to 0.51%. Their stacks overlap. New total sampled CPU was 130.85 s, and inferToMultipleTypes accounted for 4.27%, GC drain 15.32%. Profiles were collected at different times; use the matched wall samples for end-to-end performance. The new raw Sentry profile and binary/profile hashes are in benchmarks/profiles/template-index-text. With only about half a percent of Sentry CPU now in index applicability, move the large-project search to inference and broader checker work.

The numeric-fallback split restored inlining of isApplicableIndexType into findApplicableIndexInfo as verified by disassembly. Five alternating pairs versus the pre-guard shipping binary put the split at 0.968x for TypeORM and 0.960x for tRPC. Those controls lack a direct head-to-head with the unsplit guard, so they do not establish its relative benefit. Keep the split experimental. The larger remaining budgets are semantic inference, comparison and GC.

On the pinned VS Code project, wide-source unions (32 or more members) made 5,155,058 close-match predicate calls and found no matches. The prefilter builds a set of source object and generic alias symbols. When no target symbol is present, it can return the original operands without entering the nested loop. It uses the original pass for every possible match, including overlap across symbol roles. A shadow run retained every original pair comparison: the exact guard covered 5,151,270 predicates in 245 VS Code passes and 24,025 in one Sentry pass. It did not trigger on TypeORM or tRPC. Checker/compiler/CLI tests, 120 native differential cases and five new fixture variants against public tsc-ts@0.0.2 passed. Predicate savings are counts, not timing ratios.

The subsequent wide-source inferToMultipleTypes loop is a different operation. Of 5,151,370 such calls on VS Code, 5,094,720 were object sources against conditional targets; just 100 calls returned at the initial no-type-variables/NoInfer guard. Hoisting that guard cannot remove this matrix. Looking at all widths, CPU stacks assigned to the innermost inferToMultipleTypes show 4.53 of 5.59 sampled seconds on Sentry entered the final object-inference path. Property inference consumed 3.01 s of this. On VS Code, 2.58 of 4.69 s entered the object path and 1.58 s of that was the unrelated-type check. Profile provenance and per-line attribution are in benchmarks/profiles/union-inference-work.

In two fresh-process pairs using matched ordinary Go builds, previous/candidate median ratios were 0.991x on VS Code, 1.056x on Sentry, 1.052x on TypeORM and 1.195x on tRPC. Both VS Code pairs removed the same class of symbol comparisons, but one was faster and one slower. The prefilter did not activate on either small workload, which limits attribution of the timing differences. This screen does not establish a project speedup from eliminating the comparisons.

The follow-up diagnostic preserved the original wide object-to-conditional calls. All 5,094,720 pairs on VS Code were absent from that inference state's visited table on entry, present after the call, and returned a priority other than the initial maximum or circularity. Across 35,380 conditional target rows, no two resolved true/false branch pointer pairs repeated within the same wide inference invocation. An observer of candidate additions, reordering, priority reset and top-level changes on the same inference state found 35,136 pairs inserted a candidate. The other 5,059,584 (99.31%) triggered none of those writes or the equal-priority candidate branch. All pairs still affected visited/priority bookkeeping. Focus on why these calls return a priority without producing a candidate, and on representing their bookkeeping efficiently; a missing candidate write alone is insufficient to skip the call. Sentry exercised two such pairs and the other projects zero. The shipping executable was rebuilt from 8d9a8c3 and matched all five original-public regression variants.

For VS Code's 5,094,720 wide object-to-conditional inferences, all 35,136 candidate-producing pairs reached an equal-quality inference through the true branch. All remaining 5,059,584 pairs propagated an existing non-maximal visited priority through the true branch. The false branch showed none of the four observed events. The original actions, candidate writes and cached-priority handling still ran. Sentry's two pairs propagated through the false branch (one fixed and one lower quality). This distinguishes a nested cache hit from a repeat of the enclosing conditional pair. The enclosing pair was new in every case.

An experimental overlay stores wide conditional target rows as priority arrays with one source ordinal map shared among targets. The generic visited table remains authoritative for entries created before a row existed. The on-stack marker and final priority are written to the same selected storage throughout a call, including recursive reentry and late row registration. All rows are discarded when an inference state is returned to its pool. Checker/compiler/CLI tests and targeted recursion, zero-priority, late-registration and recycling tests passed. Benchmark results will determine whether avoiding general pair-map insertions offsets the new lookups and arrays.

The compact visited-row prototype passed 120 differential checks. Median VS Code time rose from 31.18 to 33.19 s and median peak RSS rose from 7,626 to 7,874 MiB. Sentry rose from 29.03 to 32.30 s. These are two alternating pairs against a matched ordinary Go build on unchanged frozen inputs. TypeORM and tRPC ratios were 0.954x and 1.021x. The design stays experimental. No public-release speedup is claimed from these internal timings.

All 35,380 wide conditional target rows in pinned VS Code came from Extract<T,U> in lib.es5.d.ts line 1623. The true branch resolved to an intersection, and the false branch was never. The true-branch cache hit was deeper than the original source/true-branch pair in every one of the 5,059,584 cases. All false branches returned at the first guard. A shortcut that looks up (source, trueType) at conditional entry misses the observed reuse. Sentry's two rows instead came from selectField.tsx and had a never true branch and a type-parameter false branch.

The standalone generator in benchmarks/profiles/conditional-cache-site reproduces the real inference events with N source interfaces and N event arms. Across widths 64, 128, 256 and 512 it produced exactly N true-branch candidate events and N*(N-1) nested-priority hits, and identical clean checks in the original public npm CLI and shipping Go. At 512 arms Go took 1.021 s and public tsc-ts 1.059 s (four alternating timing pairs). A separate 1,024-arm shipping Go profile has 4.32 sampled CPU seconds. Inclusive shares include inferToConditionalType at 39.58%, typesDefinitelyUnrelated at 17.59%, and getSimplifiedConditionalType at 0.23%. These overlap and describe the reduced workload. Investigate which constituents of the true intersection trigger the cached comparison and the unrelated-property checks before trying to shortcut a conditional. This pass did not establish a new end-to-end speedup on the four target projects.

A direct-constituent trace narrows the VS Code opportunity to the first two members of the true intersection. Member zero had 5,059,584 visited hits and 35,136 new visits reaching property inference. All 5,094,720 visits to member one stopped at the initial guard. Member two had 35,136 direct visits, all new and reaching property inference. In the 64-event reduction, member two also had 4,032 new visits that concluded the objects were definitely unrelated. That last quadratic cost is specific to the reduction in this trace. Focus on batching member-zero cached priorities across sources and skipping the inert member once per target row. Any such batch has to preserve visited entries for the enclosing conditionals, circularity handling, source order and the 35,136 visits reaching property inference. The trace counts direct, non-naked member calls with unchanged source and intersection slice. Other paths are outside its coverage. [Observer](benchmarks/profiles/extract-constituents/prepare.py)

The empty-branch overlay resolves both conditional arms in their original order and avoids the generic two-target walk when one arm is never. The checker, compiler and CLI tests passed along with a branch-level oracle test covering naked type variables, fixed inferences, contravariance and priority restoration. All 120 local compiler differential tests passed. On the generated Extract input the candidate took 266 vs 286 ms at width 256, 1,074 vs 1,100 ms at width 512 and 4,392 vs 4,136 ms at width 1,024. These are four alternating fresh-process pairs against the matched ordinary Go build. Removing the empty false arm alone did not improve scaling. [Experiment](benchmarks/profiles/conditional-never/prepare.py)

On full-project checks the empty-branch candidate showed ratios of medians of 1.059x VS Code, 1.065x Sentry, 1.035x TypeORM and 1.070x tRPC against matched ordinary Go. Both large projects had one faster pair and one slower pair. All checks returned clean diagnostics on the same input hashes. Keep the shortcut experimental. [Raw timing pairs](benchmarks/profiles/conditional-never/screen.json.gz)

Whole-project CPU budgets change the priority. Conditional inference was on 1.11% of sampled VS Code stacks and 0.86% of Sentry stacks, versus 39.58% in the generated 1,024-event case. Resolving an instantiated symbol's type was on 4.03% of VS Code, 4.42% of Sentry, 1.99% of TypeORM and 5.61% of tRPC stacks. On Sentry, alias instantiation was on 5.75% and mapped member resolution on 3.29%. These are inclusive shares from the retained shipping profiles, using the post-index-guard Sentry capture. The operations can overlap. The symbol already caches its resolved type per wrapper. Measure fresh wrappers that request the same target and mapper, their resulting type identity and their effects before attempting to share results. This is a higher cross-project priority than speeding up the rare wide Extract pattern. [CPU breakdown](benchmarks/profiles/extract-constituents/cpu.json)

A checker-local observer recorded three keys after actual cache misses inside getTypeOfInstantiatedSymbol. It compared the result pointer and counted instantiations performed during instantiateType, including its descendants. Each table holds up to 32,768 entries per checker before clearing. These completion-order records compare against the last retained request, so the numbers are observed reuse within that window. Counts in nested calls can overlap.

Across 1,191,026 VS Code requests, only two different wrappers repeated an original symbol and mapper. The other three projects had zero such repeats. Using the resolved input type and exact mapper instead exposed 204,061 repeats among 926,950 Sentry requests, accounting for 610,554 of 3,089,791 observed nested instantiations (19.76%). By comparison the share was 0.77% on VS Code, 0.58% on TypeORM and 0.63% on tRPC. All observed repeats returned the same type pointer. The simple-mapper comparison also keyed on source and target parameter values across mapper instances and observed just 12,812 nested instantiations on Sentry hits. These are counts within one resolver, and equivalent visible types do not establish equivalence of intermediate compiler state. Investigate the input shapes and concrete mapper implementations on Sentry. [Counts](benchmarks/profiles/instantiated-symbol-reuse/counts.json)

Outermost instantiated-symbol resolution spends 75% of its sampled CPU in instantiateType on VS Code and 78% on Sentry. On VS Code the largest worker child is conditional instantiation (2.75 CPU seconds); on Sentry it is instantiating a list of types (1.82 CPU seconds). The Sentry cross-wrapper observer further identifies 195,506 repeated union/ArrayTypeMapper requests accounting for 603,084 nested instantiations. Each had the same returned pointer and the same nested work count as the previous match. In contrast, 215 of the 1,324 repeated indexed-access/ArrayTypeMapper calls differed in work count. A standalone interface with N distinct properties of type string | A | B produces exactly N-1 union/array repeats and 3*(N-1) nested instantiations on those hits. Shipping Go and public tsc-ts@0.0.2 emitted byte-identical declarations at widths 64, 256 and 1,024. [Class counts](benchmarks/profiles/instantiated-symbol-reuse/classes.json) [CPU attribution](benchmarks/profiles/instantiated-symbol-reuse/cpu.json) [Reduced case](benchmarks/profiles/instantiated-symbol-reuse/reduced.json)

A Sentry-only observer classified all 195,506 union/array repeats by their directly visible union arms and checker state. Across instantiation depths, 148,490 repeats (593,455 nested instantiations) had precisely one unaliased primitive or literal arm, one other arm, no naked type parameter, no union origin or alias, and an inactive mapper at the observation point. This accounts for 98.4% of nested work in the repeated union/array group. The generated string | A | B case reproduces the repeated operation but has a different union structure. [Sentry union shapes](benchmarks/profiles/instantiated-symbol-reuse/union_shape.json)

A direct substitution experiment handles a plain parameter under an inactive, single-entry mapper. It preserves the instantiation count and the existing depth and count limits, and delegates active mappers and aliases to the ordinary path. Checker/compiler/CLI tests, focused substitution and cache tests and all 120 differential checks passed. Two alternating full-project pairs against the ordinary Go build produced median ratios of 1.031x VS Code, 0.990x Sentry, 0.914x TypeORM and 0.917x tRPC. Both small projects slowed in both orders. The shortcut remains experimental. [Measured samples](benchmarks/profiles/direct-parameter-substitution/screen.json.gz)

The repeated two-arm Sentry unions overwhelmingly represent optional React event handlers. Eighteen handler aliases paired with undefined account for 148,159 hits and 592,636 nested instantiations at four per repeat, including ReactEventHandler, MouseEventHandler and PointerEventHandler. The generated optional-handler case uses the same bivariant method alias pattern as the pinned React typings. It produces N-1 exact ReactEventHandler/undefined repeats with four nested instantiations each, and clean, byte-identical declaration output with shipping Go and public tsc-ts@0.0.2 at widths 256, 1,024 and 4,096. Target the repeated substitution of an optional aliased callable within a mapper. Before skipping it, establish the behavior of nested object and alias instantiation, active mapper entries, both instantiation limits and the canonical union result; equal output pointers alone are insufficient. [Sentry aliases](benchmarks/profiles/instantiated-symbol-reuse/union_member.json) [Reduced-case timings](benchmarks/profiles/instantiated-symbol-reuse/handlers.json)

The 4,096-handler fixture with separate declarations spent 78.57% of sampled Go CPU in getTypeAtFlowAssignment, including 70.71% in isMatchingReference. Only 0.71% was in getTypeOfInstantiatedSymbol. The same flow-assignment path accounted for 1.73% on VS Code and 0.87% on Sentry in the retained full-check profiles. Reading the same properties into one array preserved all 4,095 handler substitution repeats and 16,380 nested instantiations. Go time dropped from 1,203 to 138 ms, and public tsc-ts from 546 to 204 ms. Each timing is a median of four paired fresh checks, and both compilers agreed on declarations for each program. This changes the TypeScript input, so it isolates a workload sensitivity and is not a compiler speedup. Use the array fixture when timing handler substitution, and the separate-declaration fixture when investigating repeated assignment walks. [CPU shares](benchmarks/profiles/instantiated-symbol-reuse/flow.json) [Array control](benchmarks/profiles/instantiated-symbol-reuse/handlers_array.json)

The declaration-run experiment remembers the most recent run of unrelated ordinary variable declarations for one resolved root symbol per checker. It compares actual exported/merged symbols on a new run and stops before shared flow, matching declarations, non-statement declarations and other flow kinds. Only identifiers and plain property chains participate. Recording a symbol merge invalidates the remembered run. This preserves the normal handling of narrowing, reachability, for-in and shared nodes. A mixed shadowing/branch/reassignment fixture matched ordinary Go and the original public compiler for declarations, diagnostics and exit status in both valid and erroneous forms. All 120 differential tests passed. Among the selected upstream control-flow and narrowing cases, 142 passed and three were skipped. A 4,096-handler CPU profile went from 1.43 seconds under getTypeAtFlowAssignment to zero observed samples there. End-to-end scaling times are in PERF.md; sampling zero cannot establish zero cost. [CPU data](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/cpu.json) [Cases](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/conformance.json)

The matched ordinary Go comparisons for the declaration-run candidate gave VS Code 0.991x, Sentry 1.015x, TypeORM 1.016x and tRPC 0.994x. Both Sentry samples were faster; the other projects had a faster and a slower pair. The 8,192-handler input took 4.637 s in ordinary Go, 0.220 s in the candidate in that comparison (21.12x). Independently, public tsc-ts@0.0.2 took 1.676 s and the candidate 0.202 s on the same input (8.29x). The four-project results remain far from the goal. [Go project checks](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/screen.json.gz) [Public generated-case checks](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/public.json)

The declaration-run observer counted 886,822 declarations bypassed via earlier runs on VS Code, 403,362 on Sentry, 25,627 on TypeORM and 2,565 on tRPC. The shipping source now keeps one run beside the other per-checker flow state. The complete pinned suite checked 12,789 configurations fully and eight partially, with 2,118 inherited unsupported skips and zero failures. A permanent differential test for same-name shadowing and matching roots passed all five emit/check variants against the public npm compiler. This is a scalability fix for repeated declaration walks. Its observed benefit on the four acceptance projects remains small. [Counts](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/counts.json) [Full conformance](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/full-conformance.json)

Rebuilt the normal shipping executable at bd1baea from a clean source manifest. All 125 local differential checks passed. The original public npm CLI at 0.0.2 and the shipping Go compiler checked identical files with clean diagnostics on all four pinned projects. Public/Go median ratios from two alternating fresh pairs were 0.620x VS Code, 0.632x Sentry, 0.541x TypeORM and 0.841x tRPC. The npm launcher and its selected linux-x64-gnu executable were hashed before and after timing. [Release comparison](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/release.json.gz) [Public package identities](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/public-package.json)

With the actual shipping executable, the public/Go ratios on the handler scaling fixture were 1.72x at 1,024 names, 4.06x at 4,096, 8.72x at 8,192 and 23.96x at 16,384. The last program took 8.546 s in public tsc-ts@0.0.2 and 0.357 s in Go over four alternating pairs. Both compilers also emitted the same 1,348,970 declaration bytes from that program. This establishes an order-of-magnitude improvement on a long assignment chain; the four full-project comparisons above measure the remaining gap. [Shipping samples](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/shipping-public.json) [Declaration hash](benchmarks/profiles/flow-declaration-runs/shipping-declarations.json)

The anonymous-object instantiation observer counted 659,324 existing results in 1,115,870 Sentry probes. Of those hits, 656,718 used at most four type arguments. VS Code had 145,947 hits in 813,063 probes, TypeORM 14,063 in 66,578, and tRPC 10,830 in 25,701. In the 4,096-property handler array, every additional property after the first produced one existing single-argument result with a prior mapper and synthesized alias. [Counts](benchmarks/profiles/object-instantiation-hits/counts.json)

A second probe traced the actual workers for optional unaliased unions of undefined and an object carrying an EventHandler alias, under array mappers. The two common completed repeat paths in Sentry had 76,585 and 71,599 calls. Both ran the union, object, reference and parameter workers, found an active-mapper cache entry, found the existing outer object instantiation and normalized the final union. The two paths differed at the reference worker: the former found an object-instantiation table entry, the latter found an interned reference. The 1,024-property generated case reproduced the second path on all 1,023 repeats. These are completed-path observations; the four workers and their side effects still run. [Traces](benchmarks/profiles/object-instantiation-hits/trace.json)

The allocation prototype keeps up to four effective arguments on the stack, applies the existing two-mapper operation directly, and creates retained arguments and an alias wrapper only on an object-table miss. An alias regression initially failed when a nested mapper replaced the input's alias. Keeping the original alias pointer fixes this history, and the corrected candidate passed it as well as ownership tests at arities 1, 2, 4, 5 and 8. Matched Go checker benchmarks on warmed instantiated objects with a prior mapper and generic alias measured four allocations per lookup in the control versus one at arities 1, 2 and 4 and two at arity 5. At arity 1, median time went from 525 ns to 341 ns and allocation from 96 to 8 bytes per lookup. [Samples](benchmarks/profiles/object-instantiation-hits/bench.json)

On pinned Sentry, Go allocation profiles estimate 3.24 million objects and 238 MB allocated under the outermost getObjectTypeInstantiation call in the control, versus 1.55 million and 158 MB in the corrected candidate. The full profiles contain 25.48 and 19.92 million sampled estimated objects respectively. These are independent runs and the runtime allocation profile can lag recent allocations. Attribution under this function was 2.68% and 2.80% of sampled CPU respectively. Use the uninstrumented paired screen to judge wall time. [Attribution](benchmarks/profiles/object-instantiation-hits/attribution.json) [Raw profile identities](benchmarks/profiles/object-instantiation-hits/profiles.json)

The Sentry control's chooseOverload call tree accounts for 64.33 of 158.72 sampled CPU seconds. Inside JSX attribute construction, the expression check at jsx.go line 740 accounts for 13.33 seconds, compared with 0.93 seconds looking up the contextual property for its deprecation suggestion. JSX contextual union discrimination consumes 8.08 seconds, including 2.98 seconds filtering optional absent members and 4.71 seconds narrowing constituents. In the narrowing function across all callers, the property-or-index lookup takes 3.82 seconds and discriminator matching 0.98 seconds. These nested budgets overlap. Measure whether multiple JSX nodes ask the same union to narrow on the same ordered absent names; this could bypass an entire constituent-by-property traversal. [Control CPU profile](benchmarks/profiles/object-instantiation-hits/sentry-control-cpu.pb.gz)

The corrected allocation candidate passed all 125 CLI differential checks. Each of two full-project pairs used matched ordinary Go builds, identical ordered inputs and clean diagnostics. Median control/candidate ratios were 0.978x VS Code, 1.020x Sentry, 0.986x TypeORM and 1.016x tRPC. VS Code and TypeORM were slower in both orders. Sentry and tRPC each had one faster and one slower pair. Keep the allocation change as an experiment. [Wall times and RSS](benchmarks/profiles/object-instantiation-hits/screen.json.gz) [Differential tests](benchmarks/profiles/object-instantiation-hits/parity.log.gz)

On two independent diagnostic runs of pinned Sentry, JSX narrowing requests with no explicit discriminant and the same ordered nonempty absent-name list as the last completed absent-only request for that checker and contextual union repeated 14,997 times. They accounted for 9,659,509 of 11,093,913 nested property-or-index queries counted across JSX narrowing calls (87.1%). Of these repeated passes, 1,329 narrowed the input union. Every result matched its previous pointer. The second observer measured zero repeats creating types, symbols or instantiations, changing assignable/subtype table size, or entering under active instantiation. First encounters and explicit discriminants did show effects. Both builds executed every lookup. A four-element JSX fixture reproduces two repeats, one first encounter and one explicit discriminant. [Counts and workload](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/counts.json) [Fixture](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/probe.tsx)

The first Sentry target is reuse of a completed narrowing for an identical ordered absent-name list after the existing node/union cache misses. A correct implementation needs an eligibility rule or invalidation for mutable type/symbol resolution, relation flags and diagnostics. The counters omit these effects and the 87.1% is a share of calls in this specific traversal. For VS Code, the broader opportunity is eliminating the quadratic Extract inference walk already reproduced in conditional-cache-site. The previous compact visited-row experiment retained the walk and lost time. Target the repeated priority propagation while preserving candidate order and circularity.

An isolated JSX prototype reuses the last completed absent-only narrowing for each union when the exact ordered member-symbol pointers match and no instantiation or mapper is active. It still performs the original member filtering and writes the normal node-specific cache. On pinned Sentry the ordinary Go median fell from 22.330 s to 20.911 s over four alternating pairs on identical inputs with clean diagnostics. The candidate won three pairs. On a generated union with 32 branches and 32 discriminants, 1,024 elements took 197 ms in ordinary Go, 111 ms in the prototype and 241 ms in public npm tsc-ts@0.0.2 (three fresh processes per engine). Valid and erroneous declaration emissions and diagnostics agreed byte for byte at three widths. This comparison measures potential reuse. The prototype has no invalidation for changes to constituent property resolutions. [Sentry pairs](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/replay-sentry.json.gz) [Generated workloads](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/scaling.json)

The replay prototype passed 125 CLI differential checks. A separate internal state test of the original narrowing function changed a constituent property's resolved type while retaining the same union and discriminator symbols. The ordinary answer changed from one branch to both. This constructed checker history demonstrates what the key omits; reachability from a TypeScript source program is unverified. [History test](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/history_test.go.txt) [CLI checks](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/replay-parity.log.gz)

The pinned Sentry observer examined the original property's completed non-augmented intersection cache before each of 10,036,117 absent-only JSX lookups. For 4,954,752 the mapped property's resolved type was already the checker’s undefined type. A further 4,936,664 had a resolved union containing that exact type. Every previously resolved pointer observed matched the ordinary lookup. Only 101 entire passes (204 lookups) contained exclusively those mapped positive matches. Optimize individual matches and preserve the order and effects of exceptions within each pass. The per-lookup prototype checks the current intersection flags, apparent identity, preferred property entry, symbol dispatch flags and current resolved value before skipping the ordinary positive test. It retains no cross-node result. A focused regression passed after changing resolved values, replacing the cache entry and varying never/reduction, partial and symbol-dispatch flags. [Cell counts](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/cells.json) [Prototype](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/resolved.py)

Revalidating each positive mapped property independently preserved the original property-by-constituent traversal. On Sentry the ordinary Go and prototype medians were 31.198 s and 31.191 s across four pairs, with two wins for each. The comparison with cross-node replay ran separately, so compare each candidate to its own paired control. A generated mapped union at 1,024 elements exercises 524,288 already-resolved union-with-undefined lookups and 16,384 other nonstructured mapped lookups. This gives a focused workload with confirmed operation counts. [Sentry timing](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/resolved-sentry.json.gz) [Generated fixture coverage](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/mapped-fixture.json)

Grouping the Sentry queries by each absent property within each pass changes the optimization target. For 3,862,203 property checks, all still-eligible union constituents had an already-resolved mapped property accepting undefined. Those checks accounted for 9,852,566 lookups (98.17% of all absent-only JSX lookups); each covered only 2.55 constituents on average. There were 26,415 mixed checks and 35,157 with zero such matches. An all-positive column cannot eliminate any currently eligible constituent. Before reusing that fact for a later element, establish whether the earlier check covered the entire union or only survivors, and whether its property resolution and positive relation results still hold. Distinct mapped symbols within the all-positive columns sum to 7,724,636, limiting opportunities to deduplicate by symbol identity within a single column. [Column counts](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/columns.json)

The current-state candidate passed all 125 differential checks. Its median for 1,024 generated mapped JSX elements was 268 ms, versus 353 ms for matched ordinary Go and 361 ms for public npm tsc-ts@0.0.2. At widths 16, 256 and 1,024 both Go builds and public npm matched valid and erroneous diagnostics, exit statuses and declaration bytes. Each timed width has three rotated fresh runs per engine. [Project parity](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/resolved-parity.log.gz) [Generated samples](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/mapped-scaling.json)

On the other three acceptance projects, two matched ordinary Go pairs gave control/candidate median ratios of 0.929x VS Code, 0.855x TypeORM and 0.972x tRPC for the per-property shortcut. VS Code split the two pairs; both TypeORM and tRPC pairs were slower. The Sentry median was effectively unchanged, so this candidate stays out of the shipping compiler. The timing variation on projects with little discrimination work does not identify a cause. [Samples](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/resolved-projects.json.gz)

A fresh Sentry observer remembered a property only after every original constituent of its contextual union had been visited and had a resolved mapped type accepting undefined. It keyed that observation by the checker, union pointer and absent member-symbol pointer. After 23,603 first observations, it predicted 3,838,574 later positive property checks covering 9,795,416 of the 10,036,117 lookups (97.60%). Every predicted check ran the original algorithm; zero encountered a nonmatching or unresolved property. This addresses the survivor-subset issue for coverage. The previous state-history test still requires a correctness argument for each retained lookup. The next implementation target is a per-union list of absent properties whose completed mapped resolutions cannot narrow any constituent, retaining ordinary lookups for the remaining names and first-time resolutions. [Full-union coverage](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/neutral.json)

A prototype groups consecutive absent JSX properties into spans. It records a span only after finding a completed mapped property accepting undefined on every original union constituent. Each span is checked immediately before its turn in the narrowing loop, since ordinary checks between spans can change state. The first implementation rereads every preferred property map entry and resolved value. With two alternating pairs, pinned Sentry took 23.122 s in ordinary Go and 23.337 s with the prototype. A state test covered changed links and replaced map entries. A second implementation retains links, checks their values at each span, and clears plans before a reentrant lookup replaces a preferred property map entry. Its Go checker, compiler and CLI package tests passed. [Sentry samples](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups-map-validation.json.gz) [Grouped implementation](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups.go.txt) [Retained links](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups_links.go.txt)

The retained-link validation candidate brought the Sentry median from 26.648 s to 25.835 s across four alternating ordinary-Go pairs. Two pairs were within 0.2%, one favored the candidate by 4% and one by 13% in control/candidate ratios. All 125 CLI comparisons passed. This remains an experimental result. To remove the remaining repeated checks, the next variant marks mapped links only when their current type accepts undefined, and invalidates plans if a marked link is assigned a different value. Go type information identifies 59 direct assignments to ValueSymbolLinks.resolvedType in five production files, including the service that clears and restores types during signature help. [Sentry measurements](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups-links.json.gz) [Parity](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups-links-parity.log.gz)

The write-invalidated candidate passed the checker, compiler and CLI package tests, including a mutation between two otherwise skippable spans, plus all 125 CLI differential checks. In two alternating ordinary-Go pairs its median Sentry time fell from 26.199 s to 23.255 s (1.127x), with both pairs faster. VS Code rose from 35.262 s to 37.777 s, TypeORM moved from 2.594 s to 2.566 s, and tRPC rose from 510 ms to 602 ms. VS Code and tRPC were slower in both orders. A release decision requires accounting for these regressions. [Project samples](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups-watched.json.gz) [Parity](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups-watched-parity.log.gz)

The instrumented write-invalidated build skipped 9,484,752 actual property lookups in 32,817 spans on pinned Sentry. It built 560 plans. In this run neither a watched value change nor a preferred-entry replacement invalidated one. The four-branch fixture skipped exactly 1,024 lookups per additional JSX element, reaching 1,047,552 at width 1,024. At that width ordinary Go took 329 ms, the candidate 183 ms, and public npm tsc-ts@0.0.2 took 238 ms. At all three measured widths the engines agreed on diagnostics, status and declaration bytes for both valid input and a deliberate type error. [Skipped work](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/group-counts.json) [Timings and declaration hashes](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups-scaling.json)

Ordinary Go and the write-invalidated candidate returned byte-identical editor-API histories across seven repeated or out-of-order queries on the same checker. Each step requested a JSX attribute’s contextual type, requested signature help inside an attribute, then requested file diagnostics. Both engines returned string or number according to the discriminated branch, one signature-help candidate and diagnostic TS2322 at offset 587 for the deliberate error. [Recorded histories](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/api-history.json) [Stateful test](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/api_test.go.txt)

Against the original public npm tsc-ts@0.0.2 launcher, pinned Sentry took 15.273 s in the public compiler and 28.590 s in the write-invalidated Go candidate. Both checked the same 13,290 files with clean diagnostics in two alternating pairs. The npm launcher and native platform executable matched their release hashes before and after measurement. The observer built zero narrowing plans on VS Code, TypeORM and tRPC. Those workloads therefore receive no benefit from this shortcut; isolating the cost of touching shared write sites requires a separate ablation. The candidate remains experimental. [Public comparison](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups-public.json.gz) [Release identity](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/groups-public-package.json) [Other-project coverage](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/group-counts-other.json)

A write-only build applies the same 59 invalidation checks and checker fields as the grouped JSX prototype. It neither records a watched property nor changes the narrowing or publication paths. Across eight alternating pairs after a warmup, isolated guards gave median ordinary/guard ratios of 1.003x on tRPC and 0.987x on TypeORM. The medians of the individual paired ratios were 0.983x and 0.976x respectively. In a separate eight-pair comparison, adding the full candidate to the write-only build yielded 0.959x on tRPC (four candidate wins) and 0.953x on TypeORM (three candidate wins). These checks used the same pinned files and had clean diagnostics. The prior tRPC regression does not isolate the cost of writes. [Guards alone](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/write-guards.json.gz) [Full candidate against guards](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/add-reuse.json.gz)

On the two large projects, isolating symbol-write invalidation yielded one faster and one slower pair on each. Median ordinary/guard ratios were 0.985x on VS Code and 1.031x on Sentry. Offline profile analysis on this host overlapped the timing screen. Neither these runs nor the eight-pair small-project comparisons establish a systematic write-guard cost. [Large-project samples](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-absent-discriminants/write-guards-large.json.gz)

Single non-generic signatures account for a substantial fraction of applicability work on both large projects.
