VS Code spent 5.97 CPU seconds constructing contextual object literal arguments within that branch. Sentry spent 20.95 CPU seconds in the JSX applicability handler. The newer Sentry control also spent 6.59 CPU seconds asking for a JSX attribute's contextual type during const-context determination. This is the lead for a same-invocation lookup observer. [Profile breakdown](benchmarks/profiles/shipped-cpu-costs/PROFILE.md)

The mutable-location observer records the first contextual type only when checking that exact node for a const type parameter. If widening later asks for the node's context again during the same invocation, it performs the ordinary lookup and compares pointer identity and compiler counters around that second lookup. On the four clean full checks, all 1,482,765 repeated lookups returned the same type pointer. On VS Code, 7,291 repeats nevertheless instantiated a type and 1,367 created a type. On Sentry, 1,647 instantiated and 4,775 created a type. These counts describe queries, and categories may overlap. The 1,024-element quoted-attribute fixture produced 1,024 repeats with no observed counter changes. [Counts](benchmarks/profiles/mutable-location-context/counts.json) [Fixture](benchmarks/profiles/mutable-location-context/fixture.json)

In the newer Sentry control profile, the innermost mutable-location check spent 6.56 CPU seconds in JSX attribute contextual lookup during const detection and 2.66 during widening. Of these, 5.83 and 1.39 seconds respectively went into discriminating the JSX attribute union. A shortcut limited to the second lookup targets only a subset of 2.66/158.72 sampled CPU seconds. Speeding discrimination itself can reach both phases. These budgets overlap earlier JSX measurements and cannot be added to the measured benefit from grouped absent properties. [Source-stack breakdown](benchmarks/profiles/mutable-location-context/cpu.json)

On full Sentry, a second observer restricted to quoted JSX attributes counted 56,138 widening lookups, every one a repeat returning the same pointer. During those repeats 3,833 queries created at least one type, three triggered instantiation, and three changed the instantiation budget. In contrast all four fixture sizes showed zero changes to those counters. The literal syntax alone is insufficient to justify removing the second lookup. The better target is work shared by the first and second lookups inside JSX union discrimination, while preserving lazy type construction. [Full Sentry](benchmarks/profiles/mutable-location-context/quoted-jsx.json) [Fixture](benchmarks/profiles/mutable-location-context/quoted-fixture.json)

A JSX filter prototype remembers which absent optional properties are discriminants after a full filter has run. It checks the current explicit attribute names, semantic children when relevant, the property-list identity and the name of the JSX children property. It declines to remember provisional negative classifications and invalidates a union's filter before a recursive augmented-property replacement. Explicit-property tests, initial type resolution and the subsequent narrowing traversal run normally. The candidate passed Go checker, compiler and CLI package tests and all 125 local differential comparisons. A separate two-case test changed and restored attribute names and children and compared diagnostics and declaration bytes with ordinary Go and the native compiler. [Implementation](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-member-filter/filter.go.txt) [Shape checks](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-member-filter/history.json) [Parity](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-member-filter/parity.log.gz)

The completed JSX filter was slower on both Sentry pairs. Ordinary Go took 28.807 and 40.838 seconds, versus 39.936 and 41.201 seconds with the filter. Median ordinary/candidate was 0.858x. The two ordinary samples varied by 12.03 seconds and RSS moved in opposite directions across pairs. Both Go binaries used the same toolchain, architecture level and dependency versions; only the prototype carried Git revision metadata. The filter remains outside the production compiler. [Paired samples](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-member-filter/sentry.json.gz)

A source-line attribution separates the two operations on the original absent-member filter line. On the matched Sentry control, 1.82 of 2.98 sampled CPU seconds discovered the complete member list, an operation the prototype still executes. Filtering that list accounted for the remaining 1.16 seconds, or 0.73% of the 158.72-second profile. This narrows the CPU budget for this prototype. Optimizing the filtering loop alone has little leverage on the full check. [Attribution](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-member-filter/cpu.json)

The observer skipped 1,018,198 member predicate calls on Sentry, but reused a filter on only 3,307 of 21,213 eligible requests (15.6%). It built 10,002 filters and declined 7,904 because a negative discriminant could still be provisional. VS Code, TypeORM and tRPC had zero eligible requests. The state test separately exercised a provisional negative followed by a completed positive and rejected changed property lists, explicit names and JSX children names. Together with the 0.73% CPU filtering budget and the paired timings, this rules out advancing this particular filter for a cross-project speedup. [Coverage](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-member-filter/coverage.json) [State test](benchmarks/profiles/jsx-member-filter/filter_test.go.txt)

Shipped full-project CPU profiles put union/intersection property construction at 3.62% of sampled Sentry CPU, 1.75% on TypeORM, 0.83% on VS Code and one sample (0.35%) on tRPC. Each sample is charged to its innermost construction. Enumerating all properties was the largest immediate source on Sentry (3.28 CPU seconds), whereas checking for a discriminant was largest on VS Code (0.80 seconds). The ordinary object's reduced apparent type provides a shared, narrow fast path in ten checker call sites. For exactly TypeFlagsObject without ObjectFlagsMapped, both reductions and the apparent-type operation return the original object without invoking another semantic operation. The prototype inlines that check and leaves every other case on the original path. GNU disassembly confirms the guard is inlined in getPropertyOfTypeEx. Direct leaf samples in reduced apparent type and its two immediate helpers account for 0.72% of all Sentry CPU and 0.34% of VS Code CPU, including inputs outside the guard. [CPU breakdown](benchmarks/profiles/union-property-construction/cpu.json) [Prototype](benchmarks/profiles/union-property-construction/prepare.py)

The plain-object candidate passed Go checker, compiler and CLI package tests and all 125 differential checks. The native compiler, ordinary Go and candidate also agreed on emitted declarations for constrained object generics, a mapped tuple, recursive references and a narrowing union, including TS2339 on an impossible intersection. [Fixture and binary hashes](benchmarks/profiles/union-property-construction/fallback.json) [Parity](benchmarks/profiles/union-property-construction/parity.log.gz)

With two alternating pairs on each frozen project the plain-object guard moved median ordinary/experimental time from 39.863 s to 40.270 s on VS Code, 28.643 s to 28.184 s on Sentry, 3.789 s to 4.985 s on TypeORM and 692 ms to 639 ms on tRPC. Each large project split one pair in each direction. The first TypeORM candidate sample was 5.921 s and the second 4.048 s. All inputs and diagnostics matched. This screen compares Go builds; it does not establish a public-release speedup. [Full-project screen](benchmarks/profiles/union-property-construction/screen.json.gz)

Plain objects qualified for the reduction guard on 262,962/307,141 tRPC calls, 2,225,989/2,388,172 TypeORM calls, 39,078,457/42,613,075 VS Code calls and 68,764,188/86,646,119 Sentry calls. The high coverage removes a few flag tests per call. The observer also counted the cost of selecting a constituent to skip while determining whether an intersection is never. On Sentry, 8,176 initially unresolved mapped-object requests in that heuristic encompassed 380,338 instantiations and 364,196 new symbols. These per-call deltas can overlap when resolutions nest. In the recent Sentry control profile, 2.62 of 2.66 sampled CPU seconds in the selection helper went to resolving the constituent's structured members. [Counters](benchmarks/profiles/union-property-construction/counts.json) [CPU profile](benchmarks/profiles/object-instantiation-hits/sentry-control-cpu.pb.gz)

The same observer tracked individual newly created mapped symbols under those heuristic-driven resolutions and recorded the first subsequent call to getTypeOfMappedSymbol. It dropped zero records on the four projects. The accessor was never reached for 115,051/296,520 recorded Sentry symbols, 15,932/21,616 on VS Code, 9,480/10,445 on tRPC and 353/711 on TypeORM. This measures type access only. Name lookup, enumeration, symbol identity and service consumers can still require those symbols. A controlled intersection with an explicit key union or keyof an interface creates one symbol per key in either form. At widths 16, 256 and 1,024, reading an unrelated property, one mapped property or every mapped property yields respectively zero, one or width distinct type accesses. This supplies both a sparse reproducer and a dense control for evaluating deferred member materialization. [Scaling cases](benchmarks/profiles/union-property-construction/heuristic-fixture.json)

The eight-pair small-project repeat after one warmup reversed both initial median directions. TypeORM went from 2,790 to 2,714 ms (1.028x, five candidate wins, median paired ratio 1.047x). On tRPC the separate medians were 525 and 573 ms (0.916x), while six of eight individual pairs favored the candidate and their median ratio was 1.036x. Together with the split large-project results this provides insufficient evidence for a broad shipping improvement. The next target is the work that creates mapped members to choose an intersection constituent, including which consumers need actual symbols. [Small-project samples](benchmarks/profiles/union-property-construction/small-repeat.json.gz)

A second observer tracked mapped symbols created while resolving intersection constituents. For each symbol it recorded type access and successful named lookup on its mapped owner, and conservatively marked every recorded member when the owner's Properties() or Members() accessor exposed a container after publication. All four full checks had clean diagnostics and zero dropped records. Of the 296,520 tracked Sentry symbols, 205,385 were returned by name, 181,469 reached the type accessor, and 277,310 belonged to an exposed container. Only 14,720 had none of the four observations. The corresponding unobserved counts were 3,925/21,616 for VS Code, 420/10,445 for tRPC and 159/711 for TypeORM. Named and container accesses overlap; container exposure does not prove that a caller inspected every member. The probe does not cover direct internal field reads or establish that the remaining symbols may be removed. In the width-1,024 control, the unrelated-property, one-property, all-property and spread cases left 1,024, 1,023, zero and zero members unobserved respectively. [Overlap counts and fixture hashes](benchmarks/profiles/mapped-member-demand/counts.json)

A storage prototype keeps every mapped member symbol and its normal value links. Where the key and name types are the same pointer, the existing name link supplies the key during type resolution. A secondary record is still allocated for a synthetic origin or a different key. A duplicate property name promotes the old key to a separate record before the ordinary name/key union operations, so their recursive resolution sees the previous key. The candidate passed checker, compiler and CLI package tests and all 125 local differential checks. A dedicated diagnostic/emit comparison covered identical names, remapping, distinct enum types with the same string value, optional properties and spreading. The candidate matched both ordinary Go and the original native executable. [Prototype](benchmarks/profiles/mapped-member-demand/key_overlay.py) [Comparison](benchmarks/profiles/mapped-member-demand/key-parity.json) [Parity](benchmarks/profiles/mapped-member-demand/key-parity.log.gz)

The first matched screen for identical mapped keys favored the prototype in both orders on VS Code (71.414 to 62.328 s), Sentry (54.078 to 44.244 s) and TypeORM (6.900 to 4.543 s). Both tRPC orders favored the ordinary compiler (1.375 to 1.931 s). Sample size was two per engine per project and these ordinary-Go times were materially longer than in the preceding screen. Peak RSS varied in direction across orders on every project. No cross-project promotion follows from these timings. An editor-API regression exercised repeated property lookup, root symbols, type resolution and enumeration with an original property, a copied property, an implicit key and a merged remapped key. [Matched screen](benchmarks/profiles/mapped-member-demand/screen.json.gz) [API test](benchmarks/profiles/mapped-member-demand/keys_test.go.txt)

The operation counter confirms 449,461 Sentry mapped-symbol type resolutions read the key through the existing name link, versus 3,036 that needed the separate key. Of 746,648 new mapped symbols in the full run, 741,598 had identical key and name, but only 44,339 avoided a secondary entry at creation because a distinct origin still needs storage. Eighteen duplicate names promoted the previous key. VS Code avoided 5,152 entries and 98,529 key reads, TypeORM 388 entries and 7,564 reads, and tRPC 134 entries and 3,479 reads. These counts establish the mechanism, not the magnitude of elapsed changes. In particular, attributing the entire first TypeORM timing difference to those operations would require independent evidence. [Full-project counters](benchmarks/profiles/mapped-member-demand/key-counts.json)

Public npm tsc-ts@0.0.2 checked all 13,290 frozen Sentry inputs in a median 15.841 s, versus 26.180 s for the identical-key candidate (0.605x public/candidate). The original npm launcher and selected native platform executable matched the stored release hashes before and after. An independent eight-pair small-project test used one warmup and reversed the harness binary roles. Reading the results by executable identity, TypeORM went from 2.859 to 2.956 s (candidate faster in three pairs), and tRPC went from 501 to 525 ms (four wins). The initial TypeORM advantage did not repeat. Keep the change experimental. The remaining secondary records predominantly carry synthetic origins; this is distinct from repeated storage of the key type. [Public Sentry](benchmarks/profiles/mapped-member-demand/public.json.gz) [Reversed small-project samples](benchmarks/profiles/mapped-member-demand/reversed-small.json.gz)
